Actris 2.0: Asynchronous Session-Type Based Reasoning in Separation Logic
Jonas Kastberg Hinrichsen \rsuper a, Jesper Bengtson \rsuper b, Robbert Krebbers \rsuper c

Abstract
. Message passing is a useful abstraction for implementing concurrent programs. For real-world systems, however, it is often combined with other programming and concurrency paradigms, such as higher-order functions, mutable state, shared-memory concurrency, and locks. We present Actris : a logic for proving functional correctness of programs that use a combination of the aforementioned features. Actris combines the power of modern concurrent separation logics with a first-class protocol mechanism—based on session types—for reasoning about message passing in the presence of other concurrency paradigms. We show that Actris provides a suitable level of abstraction by proving functional correctness of a variety of examples, including a channel-based merge sort, a channel-based load-balancing mapper, and a variant of the map-reduce model, using concise specifications. While Actris was already presented in a conference paper (POPL’20), this paper expands the prior presentation significantly. Moreover, it extends Actris to Actris 2.0 with a notion of subprotocols —based on session-type subtyping—that permits additional flexibility when composing channel endpoints, and that takes full advantage of the asynchronous semantics of message passing in Actris. Soundness of Actris 2.0 is proven using a model of its protocol mechanism in the Iris framework. We have mechanised the theory of Actris, together with custom tactics, as well as all examples in the paper, in the Coq proof assistant.



Oct. 29, 2020Jun. 10, 2022

\usetikzlibrary
positioning

†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 18, Issue 2, Paper 16

1.
Introduction

Message-passing programs are ubiquitous in modern computer systems,
emphasising the importance of their functional correctness.
Programming languages, like Erlang, Elixir, and Go, have built-in
primitives that handle spawning of processes and intra-process
communication, while other mainstream languages, such as Java,
Scala, F#, and C#, have introduced an Actor model
HBS 73
to achieve similar
functionality. In both cases the goal remains the same—help design
reliable systems, often with close to constant up-time, using
lightweight processes that can be spawned by the hundreds of
thousands and that communicate via asynchronous message passing.

While message passing is a useful abstraction, it is not
a silver bullet of concurrent programming. In a qualitative study of
larger Scala projects Tasharofi
et al.

TDJ 13
write:

We studied 15 large, mature, and actively maintained actor programs
written in Scala and found that 80% of them mix the actor model
with another concurrency model.

In this study, 12 out of 15 projects did not entirely stick to the Actor
model, hinting that even for projects that embrace message passing,
low-level concurrency primitives like locks (
i.e.,
mutexes) still have
their place.
Tu
et al.

TLSZ 19
came to a similar conclusion when studying 6 large and
popular Go programs.
A suitable solution for reasoning about message-passing
programs should thus integrate with other programming and concurrency paradigms.

In this paper we introduce
Actris
—a concurrent separation logic for
proving functional correctness of programs that combine message passing with
other programming and concurrency paradigms.
Actris can be used to reason about programs written in a language that mimics the
important features found in aforementioned languages such as higher-order functions,
higher-order references, fork-based concurrency, locks, and primitives for
asynchronous message passing over channels.
The channels of our language are first-class and can be
sent as arguments to functions, be sent over other channels
(often referred to as delegation), and be stored in references.

Program specifications in Actris are written in an impredicative higher-order
concurrent separation logic built on top of the Iris framework

JSS+ 15
,
KJB+ 17
,
JKBD 16
,
JKJ+ 18
.
In addition to the usual features of Iris, Actris provides a notion of
dependent separation protocols
to reason about message passing over
channels, inspired by
binary session types
HVK 98
.
We show that dependent separation protocols integrate seamlessly with other concurrency
paradigms, allow delegation of resources,
support channel sharing over multiple concurrent threads using locks, and more.

1.1.
Message passing in concurrent separation logic

Over the last decade, there has been much work on
extensions of concurrent separation logic with
reasoning principles for message passing
FRS 11
,
LV 12
,
CKC 15
,
OBH 16
.
These logics typically include some form of mechanism for writing
protocol specifications in a high-level manner,
to elegantly reason about message passing in some specific context.

In a different line of work, researchers have developed more expressive
extensions of concurrent separation logic that support proving strong
specifications of programs involving features such as
higher-order functions, fine-grained shared-memory concurrency, and locks.
Examples of such logics are TaDA
dRPDG 14
, iCAP
SB 14
,
Iris
JSS+ 15
, FCSL
NLSD 14
, and
VST
App 14
.
However, only a few variants and extensions of these logics provide a
high-level reasoning mechanism specific to message-passing concurrency.

First off, there has been work on the use of Iris-like separation logic to reason about programs that communicate via message passing over a network.
The reasoning principles in such logics are geared towards different programming patterns than the ones used in high-level languages like Erlang, Elixir, Go, and Scala.
Namely, on networks all data must be serialised, and packets can be lost or delivered out of order.
In high-level languages messages cannot get lost, are ensured to be delivered in order, and are allowed to contain many types of data, including functions, references, and even channel endpoints.
Two examples of network logics are Disel by Sergey
et al.

SWT 18
and
Aneris by Krogh-Jespersen
et al.

KTO+ 20
.
Additionally, there has been work on the use of separation logic to prove compiler correctness of high-level message-passing languages.
Tassarotti
et al.

TJH 17
verified a small compiler of a session-typed language into a language where channel buffers are modelled on the heap.

The primary reasoning principle to model the interaction between processes in
the aforementioned
logics is the notion of a State Transition
System (STS).
As a simple example, consider the following program, which is
borrowed from Tassarotti
et al.

TJH 17
:

{\mathit{prog}}_{1}:=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ 42\right\};\texttt{recv}\ c

This program creates two channel endpoints
c
and
c^{\prime}
, forks off a
new thread, and sends the number
42
over the channel
c^{\prime}
, which is then
received by the initiating thread.
Modelling the behaviour of this program in an STS typically requires three states:

\mathsf{Init}
\mathsf{Sent}
\mathsf{Received}

The three states model that no message has been
sent (
\mathsf{Init}
), that a message has been sent but not received
(
\mathsf{Sent}
),
and finally that the message has been sent and received (
\mathsf{Received}
).
Exactly what this STS represents is made precise by the underlying logic, which
determines what constitutes a state and a transition, and how these are related
to the channel buffers.

While STSs appear like a flexible and intuitive abstraction to reason about
message-passing concurrency, they have their problems:

•

Coming up with a good STS that makes the appropriate abstractions is
difficult because
the STS has to keep track of all possible states that the channel buffers can
be in, including all possible interleavings of messages in transit.

•

While STSs used for the verification of different modules
can be composed at the level of the logic, there is no canonical way of
composing them due to their unrestrained structure.

•

Finally, STSs are first-order meaning that their states and
transitions cannot be indexed by propositions of the underlying logic, which
limits what they can express when sending messages containing functions or other channels.

1.2.
Actris 1.0: Dependent separation protocols

Actris extends separation logic with a notion called
dependent separation protocols
.
This notion is inspired by the session type community, pioneered by
Honda
et al.

HVK 98
, where channel endpoints are given types that describe
the expected exchanges.
Using session types, the channels
c
and
c^{\prime}
in the program

{\mathit{prog}}_{1}
in
Section

1.1

would have the types
c:\ \textnormal{{?}}{\mathbb{Z}}.\mathbf{end}
and

c^{\prime}:\ \textnormal{{!}}{\mathbb{Z}}.\mathbf{end}
,
where
\textnormal{{!}}T
and
\textnormal{{?}}T
denotes that a value of type
T
is sent or
received, respectively.
Moreover, the types of the channels
c
and
c^{\prime}
are
duals
—when one does a send the other
does a receive, and
vice versa
.

While session types provide a compact way of specifying the behaviour of channels,
they can only be used to talk
about the type of data that is being passed around—not their payloads.
In this paper, we build on prior work by Bocchi
et al.

BHTY 10
and
Craciun
et al.

CKC 15
to attach logical predicates to session types to say more about the payloads, thus vastly extending the expressivity.
Concretely, we port session types into separation logic in the
form of a construct
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
, which denotes ownership of a channel

c
with dependent separation protocol
\mathit{prot}
.
Dependent separation protocols
\mathit{prot}
are streams of

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
constructors that are either infinite or finite,
where finite streams are ultimately terminated by an
\mathbf{end}
constructor.
Here,
v
is the value that is
being sent or received,
P
is a separation logic proposition denoting
the ownership of the resources being transferred as part of the message, and the variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
bind into
v
,
P
, and
\mathit{prot}
.
The dependent separation protocols for the above example are:

c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle 42\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\quad\mathtt{and}\quad c^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle 42\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}

These protocols state that the endpoint
c
expects the number
42
to
be sent along it, and that the endpoint
c^{\prime}
expects to send the number

42
.
Using this protocol, we can prove that
{\mathit{p}rog}_{1}

has the specification

{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace\mathit{prog}_{1}\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace v=42\right\}}
, where
v
is its
resulting value.

Dependent separation protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
are
dependent
, meaning that
the tail
\mathit{prot}
can be defined in terms of the previously bound variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
.
A sample program showing the use of such dependency is:

{\mathit{prog}_{2}}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{x}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace{\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ (x+2)}\right\};\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ 40;\ \texttt{recv}\ c\end{array}

In this program, the main thread sends the number
40
to the forked-off thread,
which then adds two to it, and sends it back.
This program has the same specification as
{\mathit{p}rog}_{1}
, while we change the
dependent separation protocol as follows (we omit the dependent separation protocol for the dual endpoint
c^{\prime}
):

c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}}

This protocol states that the second exchanged value is exactly the first with two
added to it.
To do so, it makes use of a dependency on the variable
x
, which is
used to describe the contents of the first message, which the second message then
depends on.
This variable is bound in the protocol and it is instantiated only when a message is sent.
This is different from the logic by Craciun
et al.

CKC 15
, which does not support dependent protocols.
Their logic is limited to protocols analogous to
\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}
where
x
is free, which means the value of
x
must be known when the protocol is created.

While the prior examples could have been type-checked and verified using
the formalisms of Bocchi
et al.

BHTY 10
and Craciun
et al.

CKC 15
,
the following stateful example cannot:

{\mathit{prog}_{3}}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{l}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace l\leftarrow(\mathop{!}{l}+2);\ \texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ ()\right\};\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l\mathrel{:=}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{40}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c\ l;\ \texttt{recv}\ c;\ \mathop{!}l\end{array}

Here, the main thread stores the value
40
on the heap, and sends a
reference
l
over the channel
c
to the forked-off thread.
The main thread then awaits a signal
()
, notifying that the
reference has been updated to
42
by the forked-off thread.
This program has the same specification as
{\mathit{p}rog}_{1}
and
{\mathit{p}rog}_{2}
, but the dependent separation protocol is updated:

c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\ (x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\mapsto x\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\mapsto(x+2)\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}

This protocol denotes that the endpoints first exchange a reference
\ell
, as well as a

points-to
connective
\ell\mapsto x
that describes the ownership and
value of the reference
\ell
.
To perform the exchange
c
has to give up ownership of the
location, while
c^{\prime}
acquires it—which is why it can then safely
update the received location to
42
before sending the ownership back along with the
notification
()
.

The type system by Bocchi
et al.

BHTY 10
cannot verify this program because it does not support mutable state, while Actris can verify the program because it is a separation logic.
The logic by Craciun
et al.

CKC 15
cannot verify this program because it does not support dependent protocols, which are crucial here as they make it possible to
delay picking the location
\ell
used in the protocol until the send operation is performed.

Dependent protocols are also useful to define recursive protocols to reason about programs that use a channel in a loop.
Consider the following variant of
{\mathit{p}rog}_{1}
:

{\mathit{prog}_{4}}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{\mathit{go}\ ()}\mathrel{:=}{\big(\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ ({\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}+2});\ \mathit{go}\ ()\big)}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\mathit{go}\ ()\right\};\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ 18;\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{recv}\ c}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ 20;\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{recv}\ c}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace x+y\end{array}

The forked-off thread will repeatedly interleave receiving values with
sending those values back incremented by two.
The program
{\mathit{p}rog}_{4}
has the same specification as before, but now we use the following recursive dependent separation protocol:

c\rightarrowtail\mu\mathit{rec}.\thinspace\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathit{rec}

This protocol expresses that it is possible to make repeated exchanges with the forked-off thread to increment a number by two.
The fact that the variable
x
is bound in the protocol is once again crucial—it allows the use of different numbers for each exchange.

Furthermore, Actris inherently includes some features of conventional
session types.
One such example is the
delegation
of channels as seen in the following program:

{\mathit{p}rog}_{5}:={}\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c_{1}^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c_{1}^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c\ y;\ \texttt{send}\ c_{1}^{\prime}\ ()\right\};\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{x}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c_{2}^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace{\texttt{send}\ c_{2}^{\prime}\ (x+2)}\right\};\\
\texttt{send}\ c_{1}\ c_{2};\ \texttt{send}\ c_{1}\ 40;\ \texttt{recv}\ c_{1};\ \texttt{recv}\ c_{2}\end{array}

This program uses the channel pair
c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime}
to exchange the number
40
with the second forked-off thread, which adds
2
to it, and sends it back.
Contrary to the programs we have seen before, it uses the
additional channel pair
c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime}
to delegate the endpoint

c_{2}
to the first forked-off thread, which then sends the number over
c_{2}
.
While this program is intricate, the following dependent separation protocols describe the communication concisely:

\displaystyle c_{1}{}

\displaystyle\rightarrowtail\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\textnormal{{!}}\,(c:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle c\rangle{\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}}}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{!}}\,(y:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle y\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\end{array}

\displaystyle c_{2}{}

\displaystyle\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}

The first protocol states that the initial value sent must be a channel
endpoint
c
with the protocol used in
{\mathit{p}rog}_{1}
.
This means that the main thread must give up ownership of the channel endpoint

c_{2}
, thereby delegating it.
The first protocol then expects a value
y
to be sent,
and finally to receive a notification
()
, along with ownership of the channel

c_{2}
, which has since taken one step by sending
y
.
Note that
c
is of the type
\mathsf{Val}
of programming language values
due to the programming language being untyped.

Lastly, the dependencies in dependent separation protocols are not limited to first-order data, but can also be used
in combination with functions.
For example:

{\mathit{p}rog}_{6}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{f}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ (\lambda\_.\thinspace f()+2)\right\};\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l\mathrel{:=}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{40}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c\ (\lambda\_.\thinspace\mathop{!}l);\ \texttt{recv}\ c\ ()\end{array}

This program exchanges a value to which
2
is added,
but postpones the evaluation by wrapping the computation in a closure.
The following protocol is used to verify this program:

\displaystyle c\rightarrowtail\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\textnormal{{!}}\,(P\,Q:\mathsf{iProp})\,(f:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle f\rangle{\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace f\;()\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace v\in\mathbb{Z}*Q(v)\right\}}}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(g:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle g\rangle{\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace g\;()\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace\exists w.\thinspace(v=w+2)*Q(w)\right\}}}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\end{array}

The send constructor (
!
) does not just bind the function value

f
, but also the precondition
P
and postcondition
Q
of its
Hoare triple.
In the second message, a Hoare triple is returned that maintains the original
pre- and postconditions, but returns an integer of two higher.
To send the function, the main thread would let
P\triangleq\ell\mapsto 40
and
Q(v)\triangleq(v=40)
, and prove

{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace(\lambda\_.\thinspace\mathop{!}\ell)\ ()\thinspace{\left\{Q\right\}}
.
This example demonstrates that the state space of dependent separation protocols can be
higher-order—it is indexed by the precondition
P
and postcondition

Q
of
f
—which means that they do not have to be agreed upon
when creating the protocol, masking the internals of the function from the forked-off thread.

It is worth noting that using dependent recursive protocols it is possible to
keep track of a history of what actions have been performed, which, as is shown in

Section

7
,
is especially useful when combining channels with locks.

1.3.
Actris 2.0: Subprotocols

While Actris 1.0’s notion of dependent separation protocols is expressive enough to specify advanced
exchanges, as indicated by the examples in the previous section,
they can only reason about interactions that are strictly dual.
In particular, the dual nature of Actris 1.0 requires that:

•

Sends (
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}
)
are matched up with receives (
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}
), and
vice versa
,

•

The logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
of matched sends and receives are the same, and,

•

The propositions
P
of matched send and receives are the same.

Reasoning about programs with a more relaxed duality principle
has been studied in the session type community,
namely in the context of
asynchronous session subtyping

MYH 09
,
MY 15
.
A subtyping relation
S_{1}\!<:S_{2}
captures that the session type
S_{2}
can be used in place of
S_{1}

when type checking a program.
Channel endpoints are then allocated with strictly dual session types, after which
either side can be weakened based on the subtyping relation.
For one, the subtyping relation captures that sends can be swapped
ahead of
receives

\textnormal{{?}}{T}.\textnormal{{!}}{U}.S<:\textnormal{{!}}{U}.\textnormal{{?}}{T}.S
.
Swapping sends ahead of receives is safe to do, as the messages are simply enqueued
into the corresponding channel buffer earlier than necessary.
The following program illustrates such a non-dual yet safe interaction:

{\mathit{prog}_{7}}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ 20;\ \texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ ({\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}+2})\right\};\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ 20;\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{recv}\ c}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{recv}\ c}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace x+y\end{array}

Here, both threads first send the value 20, which is enqueued into both of the
channel buffers, after which they receive the value of the other thread.
After this, they follow a dual behaviour, where the forked-off thread sends a value,
which the main thread receives.

In this paper, we show that dependent separation protocols are compatible with the idea of asynchronous
session subtyping.
This gives rise to
Actris 2.0
, which supports
so-called
subprotocols
.
Subprotocols are formalised by a preorder
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
,
which captures (among others) a notion of swapping sends ahead of receives
(provided that the send does not depend on the logical variables of the receive).
We can prove that
\mathit{prog}_{7}
results in
42
by picking the
following dependent separation protocols:

\begin{array}[]{@{} r @{} l l @{}}c&{}\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle 20\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}&\mathtt{and}\\
c^{\prime}&{}\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle 20\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\end{array}

While the main thread satisfies the protocol of
c
immediately, the
forked-off thread does not satisfy the protocol of
c^{\prime}
, as it sends the
first
value before receiving.
However, it is possible to weaken the protocol of
c^{\prime}
using Actris 2.0’s
notion of subprotocols:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\ } l}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle 20\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\\
\sqsubseteq&\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle 20\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\end{array}

This gives

c^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle 20\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle x+2\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}}}
.
Since the first send (with value 20) is independent of the variable

x
bound by the receive, the subprotocol relation follows immediately from
the swapping property.
Note that it is
not
possible to swap the second send
(with value
x+2
) ahead of the receive, as it does in fact depend on variable

x
bound by the receive.

In addition to allowing the verification of a larger class of programs,
Actris 2.0’s subprotocols also provide a more extensional approach to reasoning about dependent separation protocols.
This is beneficial whenever we want to reuse existing specifications that might
use a syntactically different protocol, but that nonetheless logically
entail each another.
For example, the ordering of logical variables can be changed using the subprotocol
relation:

\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})(y:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle(x,y)\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,(y:\mathbb{Z})(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle(x,y)\rangle{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}

Since the subprotocol relation is a first-class logical proposition of
Actris 2.0, it also allows the manipulation of separation logic resources,
such as moving in ownership.
For example,
we can show the following
conditional
subprotocol relation:

\begin{array}[b]{@{} l @{}}\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20\ \mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\rangle{\left\{\ell_{1}\mapsto 20*\ell_{2}\mapsto 22\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{2}:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2})\rangle{\left\{\ell_{2}\mapsto 22\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\end{array}

Here, we move the ownership of
\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20
into the protocol,
to resolve the eventual obligation of sending it, while instantiating the
logical variable
\ell_{1}
with
\ell_{1}^{\prime}
.

In addition to the demonstrated features, in the rest of this paper we show
that Actris 2.0’s subprotocol relation is capable of moving resources from one
message to another.
This gives rise to a principle similar to
framing
, known from conventional
separation logic, but applied to dependent separation protocols.
Lastly, inspired by the work of Brandt and Henglein
BH 98
, the
subprotocol relation is defined coinductively, allowing us to use the principle of Löb induction
to prove subprotocol relations for recursive protocols.

1.4.
Formal correspondence to session types

Even though Actris’s notion of dependent separation protocols is influenced by binary session types, this paper does not
provide a formal correspondence between the two systems.
However, since Actris is built on top of Iris, it forms a suitable foundation
for building logical relation models of type systems.
In related work by Hinrichsen
et al.

HLKB 21
, Actris has been used to define a logical
relations model of binary session types, with support for various forms of
polymorphism and recursion, asynchronous subtyping, references, and locks/mutexes.
Similar to RustBelt
JJKD 18
,
JJKD 21
, the work by Hinrichsen
et al.

HLKB 21
gives rise
to an extensible approach for proving type safety, which can be
used to manually prove the typing judgements of racy, but safe, programs that cannot
be type checked using only the rules of the type system.

1.5.
Contributions and outline

This paper introduces
Actris 2.0
: a higher-order impredicative concurrent
separation logic built on top of the Iris framework for reasoning about
functional correctness of programs with asynchronous message-passing that combine
higher-order functions, higher-order references, fork-based concurrency, and locks.
Concretely, this paper makes the following contributions:

•

We introduce
dependent separation protocols
inspired by affine binary session types to
model the transfer of resources (including higher-order functions) between
channel endpoints.
We show that they can be used to handle choice, recursion, and delegation
(
Sections

2
,
3
,
4
and
5
).

•

We introduce
subprotocols
inspired by asynchronous session
subtyping.
This notion relaxes duality, allowing channels to send
messages before receiving others, and gives rise to a more extensional
approach to reasoning about dependent separation protocols, providing more flexibility in the
design and reuse of protocols.
We moreover show how Löb induction is used to reason about recursive subprotocols
(
Section

6
).

•

We demonstrate the benefits obtained from building Actris on top of Iris
by showing how Iris’s support for ghost state and locks can be used to prove
functional correctness of programs using manifest
sharing,
i.e.,
channel endpoints shared by multiple parties (
Section

7
).

•

We provide a case study on Actris and its mechanisation in Coq by proving
functional correctness of a variant of the
map-reduce model by Dean and Ghemawat
DG 04
(
Section

8
).

•

We give a model of dependent separation protocols in the Iris framework to prove
safety and postcondition
validity of our Hoare triples (
Section

9
).

•

We provide a full mechanisation of Actris
HBK 21

using the interactive theorem prover Coq.
On top of our Coq mechanisation, we provide custom tactics,
which we use to mechanise all examples in the paper (
Section

10
).

1.6.
Differences from the conference version

This paper is an extension of the paper
“Actris: Session-type based reasoning in separation logic” presented at
the POPL’20 conference
HBK 20
.
In this paper we present Actris 2.0, which extends Actris 1.0 with the notion of
subprotocols.
This extension introduces new logical connectives and proof rules, but also
involves a significant overhaul of the original model and
its Coq mechanisation.
We extend the presentation of the programming language semantics, model and mechanisation
substantially, with additional details, considerations, and examples, to give a better
understanding of how Actris works and how it can be used.
Concretely, this paper includes the following extensions compared to the
conference version:

•

An overview of subprotocols in the introduction (
Section

1.3
).

•

A new background section on the programming language semantics (
Section

2
) and Iris (
Section

3
).

•

A section with an expanded overview of Actris (
Section

4
, moved from
Section

5
).

•

A new section on Actris 2.0’s notion of subprotocols
(
Section

6
).

•

An updated and expanded description of the model of Actris in
Iris (
Section

9
).

•

An extension of the section on the Coq mechanisation with sample proofs
(
Section

10
).

2.
Programming language semantics

The Iris program logic is parametric in the programming language that is used.
As a result there are multiple approaches to extend Iris with support for channels:

•

Instantiate Iris with a language that has native support
for channels.
This approach was carried out in the original Iris paper
JSS+ 15
and by Tassarotti
et al.

TJH 17
.

•

Instantiate Iris with a language that has low-level concurrency
primitives, but no native support for channels, and implement channels as a
library in that language.
This approach was carried out by Bizjak
et al.

BGKB 19
for a lock-free
implementation of channels.

In this paper we take the second approach.
We implement channels in HeapLang—the default
programming language that is shipped with Iris’s Coq development
Iri 21
.
HeapLang is an untyped functional language with high-level features such as
higher-order functions,
higher-order mutable references, fork-based concurrency, and garbage collection.
Due to these high-level features, programs written in HeapLang are reminiscent of those written in high-level programming languages with message passing like Go or Erlang.

Since HeapLang is an untyped language, safety of a program is not obtained by establishing a typing judgement, but by proving a Hoare triple in the Iris/Actris logic.
Hinrichsen
et al.

HLKB 21
show how logical relations in Actris can be used to define and
prove sound a session type system for HeapLang extended with message passing.

We proceed by describing HeapLang’s syntax (
Section

2.1
) and operational
semantics (
Section

2.2
).
We then present HeapLang’s standard library for spin locks (
Section

2.3
).
We use this lock library to implement channels (
Section

2.4
), and to write programs that
combine message passing with lock-based concurrency (
Section

7
).

2.1.
Syntax

The syntax of HeapLang is as follows:

\displaystyle v\in\mathsf{Val}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle()\mid i\mid b\mid\ell\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e\mid

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt(i\in\mathbb{Z},b\in\mathbb{B},\ell\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{2})\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace v\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace v\mid\ldots

\displaystyle e\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Expr}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle v\mid x\mid e_{1}\ e_{2}\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fst}\ e\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} snd}\ e\mid

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{e_{2}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace{e_{3}}\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{e}\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{e}\mid

\displaystyle(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} match}\thinspace{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\thinspace{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{x})}\Rightarrow{e_{2}}\mid{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{x})}\Rightarrow{e_{3}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end})\mid

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{e}\mid\mathop{!}e\mid e_{1}\leftarrow e_{2}\mid

(Mutable state)

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{e\right\}\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ e_{1}\ e_{2}\ e_{3}\mid\ldots

(Concurrency)

We elide the standard boolean and arithmetic operators such as equality, addition,
subtraction, and multiplication.
We define various notions as syntactic sugar (
i.e.,
as definitions in the
meta language by use of
\triangleq
):

\displaystyle\lambda x.\thinspace e\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{\_}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e

\displaystyle e_{1};\ e_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{\_}\mathrel{:=}{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace{e_{2}}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}e_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace e_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle(\lambda x.\thinspace e_{2})\ e_{1}

\displaystyle\texttt{skipN}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{\mathit{go}}\ {{x}}\mathrel{:=}\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace{0<x}\\
\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{\mathit{go}\ (x-1)}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace()\end{array}

We use
\_
as the anonymous binder that is not used in the body of the binding expression.
The
skipN
operation, which performs a given number of no-op program steps, is used in the implementation of channels (
Section

2.4
) for proof-related reasons (explained in
Section

9.5
).
We often write definitions as

\mathit{f}\ x_{1}\cdots\ x_{n}:=e
rather than

\mathit{f}\triangleq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f\ {x_{1}\cdots\ x_{n}}\!}\ {}\mathrel{:=}e
.
For example, we write

\texttt{skipN}\ x:={}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace{0<x}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{\texttt{skipN}\ (x-1)}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace()
.

HeapLang includes the usual operations for ML-style references.
New references can be allocated using
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{e}
,
dereferenced using
\mathop{!}e
,
and updated using
e_{1}\leftarrow e_{2}
.
Concurrency is supported via
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{e\right\}
, which spawns a new thread
e

that is executed in the background.
The language also supports atomic operations like compare-and-set (
CAS
),
which are used to implement lock-free data structures and
synchronisation primitives, such as the locks (
Section

2.3
).
HeapLang is garbage collected and thus does not have a deallocation operation.

2.2.
Operational semantics

Call-by-value evaluation contexts:

\displaystyle K\in\mathsf{Ctx}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\bullet\mid e\ K\mid K\ v\mid(e_{1},K)\mid(K,v_{2})\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fst}\ (K)\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} snd}\ (K)\mid

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace K\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace{e_{2}}\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace(K)\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(K)\mid

\displaystyle(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} match}\thinspace{K}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\thinspace{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{x})}\Rightarrow{e_{2}}\mid{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{x})}\Rightarrow{e_{3}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end})\mid

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{(}K)\mid\mathop{!}K\mid e\leftarrow K\mid K\leftarrow v\mid

(Mutable state)

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ e_{1}\ e_{2}\ K\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ e_{1}\ K\ v_{2}\mid\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ K\ v_{1}\ v_{2}\mid\ldots

(Concurrency)

Head reductions of HeapLang:

\displaystyle({(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e)(v)};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({{{e}[{v}/{x}]}[{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e)}/{f}]};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fst}\ (v_{1},v_{2})};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({v_{1}};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} snd}\ (v_{1},v_{2})};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({v_{2}};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} true}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace{e_{2}}};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({e_{1}};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{e_{1}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace{e_{2}}};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({e_{2}};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle\left({{\begin{array}[]{rll}\lx@intercol\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} match}\thinspace{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{i}\thinspace v)}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{x})}&\Rightarrow{e_{1}}\\
|&{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{x})}&\Rightarrow{e_{2}}\\
\lx@intercol\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}};{\sigma}\right)\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({{e_{i}}[{v}/{x}]};{\sigma};[\,])

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\ i\in\left\{1,2\right\}

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{v}};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({\ell};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]};[\,])

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\ \sigma(\ell)=\ \perp

\displaystyle({\mathop{!}\ell};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({v};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]};[\,])

\displaystyle({\ell\leftarrow w};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({()};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}w\right]};[\,])

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ \ell\ v^{\prime}\ w};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} true}};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}w\right]};[\,])

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\ v=v^{\prime}

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ \ell\ v^{\prime}\ w};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}};{\sigma\!\left[\ell\mathop{\leftarrow}v\right]};[\,])

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\ v\neq v^{\prime}

\displaystyle({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{e\right\}};{\sigma})\quad\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}

\displaystyle\quad({()};{\sigma};[e])

Thread-local and threadpool reductions of HeapLang:

\frac{{e_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}{e_{2}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e}}{{K{[}\,e_{1}\,{]}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tl}}{K{[}\,e_{2}\,{]}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e}}\frac{{e_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tl}}{e_{2}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e}}{{T\cdot[e_{1}]\cdot T^{\prime}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tp}}{T\cdot[e_{2}]\cdot T^{\prime}\cdot\vec{e}};{\sigma_{2}}}

Figure 1.
The operational semantics of HeapLang.

The small-step operational semantics of HeapLang is presented in
Figure

1
.
The type of program states
\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{State}
is defined as:

\sigma\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{State}\triangleq\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc}\xrightharpoonup{\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.52742pt\textsf{fin}\kern-0.21098pt$}}}\mathsf{Val}

That is, program states are finite partial maps from allocated locations to their stored values.

The
head reduction

({e_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}{e_{2}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e})

describes how an expression
e_{1}\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Expr}
in an initial program state
\sigma_{1}\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{State}
reduces to a new expression
e_{2}\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Expr}
in a possibly updated program state
\sigma_{2}\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{State}
.
Additionally, it keeps track of a list of newly spawned threads
\vec{e}\in\mathsf{List}\ \kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Expr}
.
The reduction rule

({\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{e\right\}};{\sigma})\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}({()};{\sigma};[e])

describes how a new thread
e
is spawned by adding it to the list of newly spawned threads

[e]
.
Conversely, the list of newly spawned threads is empty for all of the other
reduction rules.

The
thread-local reduction

({e_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tl}}{e_{2}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e})
lifts the head reduction to whole expressions.
It decomposes the initial expression
e_{1}
into
K{[}\,e_{1}^{\prime}\,{]}
, where
K
is a
call-by-value evaluation context

FH 92
and a head expression
e_{1}^{\prime}
.
The head expression
e_{1}^{\prime}
is then reduced, using
({e_{1}^{\prime}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{h}}{e_{2}^{\prime}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e})
, and the final expression
e_{2}
is set to
K{[}\,e_{2}^{\prime}\,{]}
.
Evaluation contexts (shown in
Figure

1
)
provide a deterministic reduction order of sub-expressions.
HeapLang reduces right-to-left, meaning that in expressions such as

{e_{1}}\leftarrow{e_{2}}
the expression
e_{2}
reduces before
e_{1}
.
This is determined by the corresponding evaluation contexts
e\leftarrow K
and

K\leftarrow v
, which state that we only evaluate sub-expressions of the
target location, once the term to store is a value.
More precisely, we would initially get

(e_{1}\leftarrow\bullet){[}\,e_{2}\,{]}
.
If
e_{2}
reduces to a value
v_{2}
the context syntax dictates that
the hole then moves to
e_{1}
yielding

(\bullet\leftarrow v_{2}){[}\,e_{1}\,{]}
.
If
e_{1}
reduces to a value
v_{1}
we finally end up with
the expression
v_{1}\leftarrow v_{2}
, as there is no context syntax where both constituents
are values, and this expression can be reduced using a standard head reduction.

Finally, the
threadpool reduction

({\vec{e}_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tp}}{\vec{e_{2}}};{\sigma_{2}})
is the top-level reduction relation that describes the interleaving of threads.
It describes how a concurrently running list of threads
\vec{e}_{1}
,
in an initial program state
\sigma_{1}
,
reduce to a new list of threads
\vec{e_{2}}
in an updated program state
\sigma_{2}
.
At each step a thread
e_{1}
is picked non-deterministically from
\vec{e}_{1}

and reduced one step to
e_{2}
via the thread-local reduction
({e_{1}};{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tl}}{e_{2}};{\sigma_{2}};\vec{e})
.
The final list of threads
\vec{e_{2}}
is obtained from
\vec{e_{1}}
by replacing the expression
e_{1}
with
e_{2}
and appending the list
\vec{e}
of newly spawned threads to the end.

We refer the interested reader
to Iris Development Team
[
Iri 21
,
docs/heap_lang.md
]

for more details on the semantics of HeapLang,
and to Jung
et al.

[
JKJ+ 18
, §6.1]

for details on the language-parametric aspects of Iris.

2.3.
Implementation of locks

\displaystyle\texttt{new\_lock}\;():={}

ref

false

\displaystyle\texttt{try\_acquire}\ \mathit{lk}:={}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} CAS}\ \mathit{lk}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} true}

\displaystyle\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}:={}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace(\texttt{try\_acquire}\ \ \mathit{lk})\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}

\displaystyle\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}:={}

\displaystyle\mathit{lk}\leftarrow\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}

Figure 2.
Implementation of locks in HeapLang.

Using HeapLang it is possible to implement various kinds of locks/mutexes.
We consider the simplest kind of lock—a spin lock—whose implementation from the HeapLang standard library is shown in
Figure

2
.

A spin lock implemented using a reference to a boolean, which is
false
if the lock is unlocked, and
true
if the lock is locked.
The
\texttt{new\_lock}\;()
operation creates a new lock
\mathit{lk}
, which is initially unlocked (
i.e.,

false
).
The operation
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
will atomically (using compare-and-set)
take the lock, or loop if the lock is already taken.
The
\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}
operation releases the lock so that it may be acquired
by other threads.

2.4.
Implementation of channels

Following the literature on asynchronous session types, the message-passing semantics of our channels is

binary
(communication is between two parties),

asynchronous
(sending messages does not block),

bidirectional
(messages can be in transit in both directions simultaneously),

reliable
(messages are never dropped), and

order preserving
(messages always arrive in the order that they were sent).

\displaystyle\texttt{new\_chan}\ ():=

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace(l,r,\mathit{lk})\mathrel{:=}(\mathtt{lnil}\ (),\mathtt{lnil}\ (),\texttt{new\_lock}\;())\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
((l,r,\mathit{lk}),(r,l,\mathit{lk}))\end{array}

\displaystyle\texttt{send}\ c\ v:=

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace(l,r,\mathit{lk})\mathrel{:=}c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\\
\quad\mathtt{lsnoc}\ l\ v;\\
\quad\texttt{skipN}\ (\mathtt{llength}\ r);\\
\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}\end{array}

\displaystyle\texttt{try\_recv}\ c:=

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace(l,r,\mathit{lk})\mathrel{:=}c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace\mathit{ret}\mathrel{:=}(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace(\mathtt{lisnil}\ r)\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace{(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{()})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathtt{lpop}\ r)}))\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk};\ \mathit{ret}\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle\texttt{recv}\ c:=

\displaystyle\ {\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} match}\thinspace(\texttt{try\_recv}\ c)\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{()}&\Rightarrow\texttt{recv}\ c\\
|&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{v}&\Rightarrow v\\
\lx@intercol\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}

Figure 3.
Implementation of bidirectional channels in HeapLang.

The implementation of our channels in HeapLang is displayed in

Figure

3
.
It uses locks (
Section

2.3
) and a linked list library.
This list library provides functions for creating a empty list (
\mathtt{lnil}
), testing
if a list is empty (
\mathtt{lisnil}
),
computing the length of a list (
\mathtt{llength}
),
adding an element to the back (
\mathtt{lsnoc}
),
and popping an element of front (
\mathtt{lpop}
).
The last two functions mutate the list, instead of creating a copy.
The implementation of the list library is standard, and hence elided.

Intuitively, the channels can be thought of as a pair of buffers

(\vec{v}_{1},\vec{v}_{2})
of unbounded size.
The
\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()
operation creates a new channel
whose buffers are empty, and returns a tuple of endpoints
(c_{1},c_{2})
.
Bidirectionality is obtained by having one endpoint receive from the others
send buffer and
vice versa
.
As such, the
\texttt{send}\ c_{i}\ v
operation enqueues the value
v
in its own
buffer,
i.e.,

\vec{v}_{i}
,
and the
\texttt{recv}\ c_{i}
operation dequeues a value from the other buffer,

i.e.,
from
\vec{v}_{2}
if
i=1
and from
\vec{v}_{1}
if
i=2
.
The message passing is asynchronous, as
\texttt{send}\ c\ v
will always
reduce, while
\texttt{recv}\ c
will loop as long as the receiving buffer is empty.

More specifically, the
new_chan
function creates new channels
by allocating two empty mutable linked lists
l
and
r
using

\mathtt{lnil}\ ()
,
along with a lock
\mathit{lk}
using
\texttt{new\_lock}\;()
, and returns the tuples

(l,r,\mathit{lk})
and
(r,l,\mathit{lk})
,
where the order of the linked
lists
l
and
r
determines the side of the endpoints.
We refer to the list in the left position as the endpoint’s own buffer,
and the list in the right position as the other endpoint’s buffer.

The
send
function sends a value
v
over a given channel endpoint

(l,r,\mathit{lk})
, by enqueueing it in the
l
buffer.
The function operates in an atomic fashion by first acquiring the lock via

\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
, thereby entering the critical section, after which the value
is enqueued (
i.e.,
appended to the end) of the endpoint’s own buffer using
the function
\mathtt{lsnoc}\ l\ v
.
The
\texttt{skipN}\ (\mathtt{llength}\ r)
instruction is a no-op that is inserted to
aid the proof.
We come back to the reason why this instruction is needed
in
Section

9.5
.

The
recv
function receives a value over a channel endpoint

(l,r,\mathit{lk})
, by dequeueing the first value in the
r

buffer.
It does so by performing a loop that repeatedly calls the helper function

try_recv
.
This helper function attempts to receive a value atomically, and fails if there
is no value in the other endpoint’s buffer.
The function
try_recv
acquires the lock with

\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
, and then checks whether the other endpoint’s buffer is empty
using
\mathtt{lisnil}\ r
.
If it is empty, nothing is returned (
i.e.,

\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace{()}
), while otherwise the value is
dequeued and returned (
i.e.,

\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathtt{lpop}\ r)}
).

Throughout the paper, we often use a combined operation for starting a thread and creating a channel between the parent and child thread:

\texttt{start}\ {f}:=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{(c,c^{\prime})}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{f\ c^{\prime}\right\};\ c

3.
The Iris logic

We give a brief introduction to the features of Iris that play an important
role in Actris:
its support for basic separation logic (
Section

3.1
),
higher-order impredicative separation logic (
Section

3.2
),
guarded recursion and step-indexing (
Section

3.3
),
and Iris’s adequacy theorem (
Section

3.4
).
This section does not present new material, so readers that are already
familiar with Iris can skip it.
An extensive overview of Iris can be found in
JKJ+ 18
, and a
tutorial-style introduction can be found in
BB 20
.

Grammar:

\displaystyle\tau,\sigma\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle x\mid 0\mid 1\mid\mathbb{B}\mid{\rm Nature}\mid\mathbb{Z}\mid\mathsf{Type}\mid\forall x:\tau.\thinspace\sigma\mid

\displaystyle\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc}\mid\mathsf{Val}\mid\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Expr}\mid\mathsf{iProp}\mid\mathsf{List}\ \tau\mid\ldots

\displaystyle t,u,P,Q\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle x\mid\lambda x:\tau.\thinspace t\mid t(u)\mid t(\tau)\mid

(Polymorphic lambda-calculus)

\displaystyle\textsf{True}\mid\textsf{False}\mid P\land Q\mid P\vee Q\mid P\Rightarrow Q\mid

(Propositional logic)

\displaystyle\forall x:\tau.\thinspace P\mid\exists x:\tau.\thinspace P\mid t=u\mid

(Higher-order logic with equality)

\displaystyle P*Q\mid P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q\mid\ell\mapsto v\mid{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace Q\right\}}\mid

(Separation logic)

\displaystyle\mu x:\tau.\thinspace t\mid\mathop{{\triangleright}}P\mid\ldots

(Guarded recursion and step indexing)

Basic affine separation logic:

\frac{P*Q}{P}\quad\textsc{lab={Affine}}\frac{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q\right\}}}{{\left\{P*R\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q*R\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-frame}}\frac{}{{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace v\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace w=v\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-val}}\frac{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}}{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{e\right\}\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace w=()\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-fork}}\frac{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace Q\right\}}\forall v.\thinspace{\left\{Q\right\}}\thinspace K{[}\,v\,{]}\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace R\right\}}}{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace K{[}\,e\,{]}\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace R\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-bind},right={$K$ a call-by-value evaluation context}}

Heap manipulation:

\frac{}{{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{v}\thinspace{\left\{\ell.\thinspace\ell\mapsto v\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-alloc}}\frac{}{{\left\{\ell\mapsto v\right\}}\thinspace\mathop{!}\ell\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace(w=v)*\ell\mapsto v\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-load}}\frac{}{{\left\{\ell\mapsto v\right\}}\thinspace\ell\leftarrow w\thinspace{\left\{\ell\mapsto w\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-store}}

Guarded recursion and step indexing:

\frac{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace{{e}[{v}/{x}]}[{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e}/{f}]\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q\right\}}}{{\left\{\mathop{{\triangleright}}P\right\}}\thinspace(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{f}\ {x}\mathrel{:=}e)\;v\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-rec}}\frac{P}{\mathop{{\triangleright}}P}\quad\textsc{lab={$\mathop{{\triangleright}}$-intro}}\frac{P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q}{\mathop{{\triangleright}}P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\mathop{{\triangleright}}Q}\quad\textsc{lab={$\mathop{{\triangleright}}$-mono}}\frac{\mathop{{\triangleright}}P\Rightarrow P}{P}\quad\textsc{lab={L\"{o}b}}\frac{}{(\mu x.\thinspace t)={t}[{\mu x.\thinspace t}/{x}]}\quad\textsc{lab={$\mu$-unfold}}

Figure 4.
The grammar and a selection of rules of Iris.

3.1.
Basic separation logic

Propositions in separation logic describe ownership of resources, and can thus intuitively be thought of as predicates over resources.
The propositions of Iris
P,Q\in\mathsf{iProp}
range over an extensible set of resources,
which includes the program state.
Iris is a higher-order separation logic, so it has the usual
logical connectives such as conjunction (
P\land Q
), implication
(
P\Rightarrow Q
), universal
(
\forall x\!:\!\tau.\thinspace P
) and existential
(
\exists x\!:\!\tau.\thinspace P
) quantification, as well as
the connectives of separation logic:

•

The
points-to connective

(\ell\mapsto v)
asserts exclusive
resource ownership of a location
\ell\in\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc}
in the program state,
stating that it holds
the value
v\in\mathsf{Val}
.

•

The
separating conjunction
(
P*Q
) states that
P
and

Q
holds for disjoint sets of resources.

•

The
separating implication

(
P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q
) states that by giving up ownership of the resources
described by
P
, we obtain ownership of the resources described by
Q
.
Separating implication is used similarly to
implication since (
P
entails
Q\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}R
)
iff (
P*Q
entails
R
).

•

The
Hoare triple

{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q\right\}}

states that if the initial program state satisfies the precondition
P
,
then (1) the expression
e
is safe (
i.e.,
does not go wrong),
and, (2) if
e
reduces to a value
v
, then the final program
state satisfies the postcondition
{Q}[{v}/{w}]
.
We often omit the binder
w
in the postcondition if the result is the
unit value
()
.

We say that an Iris proposition
P

is valid
iff it holds for all
resources,
i.e.,

P
is valid iff
True
entails
P
.
Note that
P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q
is valid iff
P
entails
Q
, so
we often use the separating implication (
\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}
) in place of entailment.
For readability, we use inference-style rules to denote
separation logic rules

(P_{1}\ast\dotsb\ast P_{n})\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q
as:

\frac{P_{1}\dots P_{n}}{Q}

Iris is an affine separation logic, which means that propositions are upwards
closed in the resources,
i.e.,

P*Q
entails
P
(rule

4
).
Affinity matches up with the use of a garbage-collected
programming language—one can simply dispose of an unused points-to connective

\ell\mapsto v
using rule
4
when a location
\ell
is no
longer referenced.

While many propositions of separation logic assert exclusive ownership of
resources (
e.g.,

\ell\mapsto v
), others do not (
e.g.,

t=u
).
Propositions that do not assert exclusive ownership enjoy some useful laws.
Separation conjunction (
P*Q
) is logically equivalent to
regular conjunction (
P\land Q
) if at least one conjunct does not
assert exclusive ownership, and separating implication
(
P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q
) is logically equivalent to regular implication
(
P\Rightarrow Q
) if the premise
P
does not
assert exclusive ownership.
1
1

1

Formally, these equivalences hold for the class of

persistent
propositions, see
[
JKJ+ 18
, §2.3]
.

For example,
(t=u)*Q
and
(t=u)\land Q

are logically equivalent.
Since separating conjunction/implication is omnipresent in Iris, we prefer the
use of separating conjunction/implication over regular conjunction/implication
if both can be used.
This is also the convention used in the Iris Coq development.

Iris’s notion of resources is not limited to locations in the program state
(
i.e.,

\ell\mapsto v
), but can
be extended with user-defined
ghost
resources.
We use ghost resources to define Actris’s connective

c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
for exclusive ownership of the channel endpoint
c

with protocol
\mathit{prot}
(
Section

9
), and
to reason about programs with non-trivial sharing (
Section

7
).

The rules for Hoare triples are mostly standard, but it is worth pointing out
the rule for
4
.
This rule enables reductions of an expression

e
, in some evaluation context
K
, based on the precedence enforced by
the evaluation contexts presented in
Section

2.2
.

3.2.
Higher-order impredicative separation logic

The Iris logic is:

•

Higher-order:

Using Iris’s quantifiers
\forall x\!:\!\tau.\thinspace P
and

\exists x\!:\!\tau.\thinspace P
it is not only possible to quantify over
first-order types (like
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{List}\ \mathbb{Z}
), but over any type, including
functions (like
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
),
higher-order functions (like
(\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}
),
polymorphic functions (like
\forall T.\thinspace\mathsf{List}\ T\to{\rm Nature}
),
Iris propositions (
\mathsf{iProp}
), and Iris predicates (like
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathsf{iProp}
).

•

Impredicative:

Iris’s logical connectives can be nested arbitrarily.
Notably,
\forall P:\mathsf{iProp}.\thinspace Q
is an Iris proposition, and not an Iris
proposition in a higher universe.
Similarly, Hoare triples

{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace Q\right\}}
and other Iris connectives
like
\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R
for lock ownership (
Section

7
)
are first-class Iris propositions themselves.

As we will see in this paper, Actris expands on Iris’s support
for higher-order impredicative separation logic by allowing the variables

\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
in the dependent separation protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
to range over any type (including
Actris’s type of protocols
\mathsf{iProto}
), and the proposition
P
to contain
any Iris/Actris connective (including the Actris connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}

for channel ownership).
This is particularly useful to reason about message-passing programs
that transfer functions (
Section

5.2
) and channels (
Section

5.5
).

To define (pure) functions and predicates used in program specifications, Iris
embeds the polymorphic lambda calculus.
In the Coq development of Iris, this lambda calculus is obtained via a shallow
embedding, and thus comprises the usual Coq data types and
functions.
2
2

2

Coq, Iris, and
Actris have a predicative
\mathsf{Type}
hierarchy, while propositions are impredicative.
For brevity’s sake, we omit details about predicativity of
\mathsf{Type}
, as they are
standard.

We should stress that Iris’s lambda calculus is different from our
programming language (HeapLang)—the former is typed and pure, whereas the
latter is untyped and impure.
Consequently, there are two kinds of lambda abstraction
(
\lambda x:\tau.\thinspace t
for Iris and
\lambda x.\thinspace e
for HeapLang).
It should be clear from context which of the lambda abstractions is used.

Figure

4
includes a subset of the Iris grammar.
The typing judgement is mostly standard and can be derived from the use of
meta variables—we use the meta variables
P
and
Q
for
propositions (type
\mathsf{iProp}
),
the meta variable
v
for values (type
\mathsf{Val}
),
and the meta variables
t
and
u
for general terms of any type.
Similar to Coq,
\lambda x:\tau.\thinspace t
is used for both term and
type abstraction, and we write
\tau\to\sigma
for
\forall x:\tau.\thinspace\sigma

if
x
is free in
\sigma
.

3.3.
Guarded recursion and step-indexing

Iris is step-indexed
AM 01
,
Ahm 04
,
meaning that propositions are indexed by a natural number—referred to as
the
step-index
—which is used to stratify a number of semantically
cyclic constructs and reasoning principles.
Iris employs the logical account of step-indexing
AMRV 07
,
DAB 11

where the step-index is implicit, and internalised in the logic through the

later modality
(
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
)
Nak 00
.
Actris and Iris use step-indexing as follows:

•

The principle of Löb induction (rule
4
) is used to reason
about (among others) recursive functions.
When proving
P
, Löb induction lets us assume that a proposition holds

later
, denoted
\mathop{{\triangleright}}P
.
The proposition
\mathop{{\triangleright}}P
is strictly weaker than
P
, since
P

entails
\mathop{{\triangleright}}P
(rule
4
), while the reverse does
not hold.
The later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) can be eliminated by taking a program step, which is
formalised by the Iris proof rule
4
.
In Actris we use Löb induction to reason about infinite protocols
(
Section

6.4
).

•

The guarded recursion operator (
\mu x:\tau.\thinspace t
) lets us construct
recursive predicates without a restriction on the variance of
x
in
t
.
Instead, the variable
x
should be
guarded
, which means that
it should appear under a
contractive
term construct.
The prime example of a contractive construct is the later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
).
The rule
4
says that
\mu x:\tau.\thinspace t
is in fact
a fixpoint of
t
.
Actris’s dependent separation protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}

are contractive in the tail argument
\mathit{prot}
, and thereby make it possible
to use Iris’s guarded recursion operator to define recursive protocols (
Section

5.4
).

•

Iris’s support for higher-order ghost state
JKBD 16
is used
to provide a model of Actris in Iris (
Section

9
).
Additionally, higher-order ghost state is used by Iris to obtain
impredicative invariants
SB 14
, which in turn are used
to prove the specification of locks
HAN 08

used in
Section

7
.

3.4.
Adequacy of Iris

The adequacy theorem of Iris connects the derivation of Hoare triples to
the operational semantics of the programming language.
A closed proof of a Hoare triple gives rise to safety and
postcondition validity.
By safety we mean that the program cannot go wrong,
e.g.,
by resolving
an illegal function application (
e.g.,

\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} true}+42
), or accessing an
invalid location (
i.e.,

\mathop{!}\ell
with
\ell\notin\textsf{dom}(\sigma)
).
Safety is defined formally as:

\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{safe}\ e\triangleq\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\forall\sigma,T,\sigma^{\prime}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}({[e]};{\sigma}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tp}}^{\ast}{T};{\sigma^{\prime}})\\
\text{implies}\ \forall e^{\prime}\in T.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}(e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Val})\ \text{or}\\
(\exists e^{\prime\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime},\vec{e}.\thinspace{e^{\prime}};{\sigma^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tl}}{e^{\prime\prime}};{\sigma^{\prime\prime};\vec{e}})\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

This definition is not concerned with whether a program
terminates (total correctness).

Postcondition validity means that if the main thread terminates with a value

v
, then the postcondition holds for that value.
This is defined formally as:

\mathsf{post\_valid}\ (e,\varphi)\triangleq\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\forall\sigma,v,T,\sigma^{\prime}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}({e};{\sigma}\xrightarrow{{}}_{\textsf{tp}}^{\ast}{[v]\cdot T};{\sigma^{\prime}})\\
\text{implies}\ (\varphi\ v)\end{array}\end{array}

Theorem 1

(Adequacy of Iris)
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be a meta-level (i.e., Coq) predicate over values and
suppose
{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace\varphi\ v\right\}}
is
derivable in Iris, then

\mathsf{safe}\ e
and

\mathsf{post\_valid}\ (e,\varphi)
.

4.
The Actris logic

Grammar:

\displaystyle\tau,\sigma\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\ldots\mid\mathsf{iProto}\mid\ldots

\displaystyle t,u,P,Q,\mathit{prot}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\ldots\mid\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\mid\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\mid\mathbf{end}\mid

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{prot}}\mid\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}\mid c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\mid\ldots

Dependent separation protocols:

\begin{aligned} \overline{\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}}={}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\overline{\mathit{prot}}}\\
\overline{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}}={}&\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\overline{\mathit{prot}}}\\
(\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}})\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}={}&\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{(\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})}\\
(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}})\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}={}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{(\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})}\\
\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot(\mathit{prot}_{2}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{3})={}&(\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})\cdot\mathit{prot}_{3}\\
\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} \overline{\mathbf{end}}={}&\mathbf{end}\\
\overline{\overline{\mathit{prot}}}={}&\mathit{prot}\\
\mathit{prot}\cdot\mathbf{end}={}&\mathit{prot}\\
\mathbf{end}\cdot\mathit{prot}={}&\mathit{prot}\\
\overline{\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}}={}&\overline{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\cdot\overline{\mathit{prot}_{2}}\end{aligned}

Message passing:

\frac{}{{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace\exists c_{1},c_{2}.\thinspace w=(c_{1},c_{2})\ast c_{1}\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\ast c_{2}\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathit{prot}}\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-new}}\frac{}{{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}*{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c\ ({v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}])\thinspace{\left\{c\rightarrowtail{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-send}}\frac{}{{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace w={v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]*c\rightarrowtail{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]*{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-recv}}

Figure 5.
The primitive constructs and proof rules of Actris 1.0.

This section describes the core features of Actris 1.0: its dependent separation protocols mechanism (
Section

4.1
), proof rules (
Section

4.2
), and
its adequacy result (
Section

4.3
).
Actris inherits all features of Iris, which is achieved by defining Actris as an
embedded logic in Iris.
This means that all of Actris’s primitive constructs are defined in Iris,
and all of Actris’s primitive proof rules are in fact lemmas in Iris.
We show how Actris is embedded in Iris in
Section

9
.

4.1.
Dependent separation protocols

The key feature of Actris is its session-type like dependent separation protocols mechanism.
Dependent separation protocols
\mathit{prot}
are streams of

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
constructors that are either infinite or finite.
The finite streams are ultimately terminated by an
\mathbf{end}
constructor.
The value
v
denotes the message that is being sent (
!
) or received
(
?
), the Iris proposition
P
denotes the ownership that is transferred
along the message, and
\mathit{prot}
denotes the protocol that describes the subsequent
messages.
The logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
can be used to bind variables in
v
,
P
,
and
\mathit{prot}
.
For example, the following dependent separation protocols expresses that a pair of a boolean and an
integer reference whose value is at least 10 is sent:
3
3

3

Note that
\ell\mapsto i\ast 10<i
is logically equivalent to

\ell\mapsto i\land 10<i
as
10<i
does not describe ownership.
As discussed in
Section

3.1
, we prefer the version with
separation conjunction.

\textnormal{{!}}\,(b:\mathbb{B})\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,(i:{\rm Nature})\,\langle(b,\ell)\rangle{\left\{\ell\mapsto i*10<i\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}

We often omit the proposition
{\left\{P\right\}}
, which simply means it is

True
.

Apart from the constructors for dependent separation protocols, Actris provides two primitive
operations,
\overline{\mathit{prot}}
and
\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}
.
The
\overline{\mathit{prot}}
operator denotes the
dual
of a protocol.
Similar to conventional session types, it transforms the protocol by changing all
sends (
!
) into receives (
?
), and
vice versa
.
Taking the dual twice thus results in the original protocol.
The operator
\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}

appends
the protocols
\mathit{prot}_{1}

and
\mathit{prot}_{2}
, which is achieved by substituting any
\mathbf{end}
in
\mathit{prot}_{1}

with
\mathit{prot}_{2}
.

Channel endpoints are ascribed with dependent separation protocols using the channel endpoint ownership
connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
, which captures unique
ownership of the channel endpoint
c
and states that the endpoint follows
the protocol
\mathit{prot}
.

4.2.
Actris’s proof rules for message passing

Actris provides proof rules for the three message passing operations

new_chan
,
send
, and
recv
(see
Section

2.4

for the definition of these operations).
The rule
5
allows ascribing any protocol to newly created
channels using
\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()
, obtaining ownership of
c_{1}\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
and

c_{2}\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathit{prot}}
for the respective endpoints.
The duality of the protocol guarantees that any receive (
?
) is matched with a
send (
!
) by the dual endpoint, which is crucial for establishing safety.

The rule
5
for
\texttt{send}\ c\ w
requires the head of the
dependent separation protocol of
c
to be a send (
!
) constructor, and
the value
w
that is sent to match up with the ascribed value.
To send a message
w
, we need to give up ownership of

c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
,
pick an appropriate instantiation
\vec{t}
for the variables

\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
so that
w={v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
,
give up ownership of the associated resources
{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
,
and finally regain ownership of the protocol tail

c\rightarrowtail{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
.

The rule
5
for
\texttt{recv}\ c
is essentially dual to the rule

5
.
We need to give up ownership of

c\rightarrowtail\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
, and
in return acquire the resources
{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]
,
the return value
w
where
w={v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]
,
and finally the ownership of the protocol tail

c\rightarrowtail{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]
,
where
\vec{y}
is some instantiation of the protocol variables.

Finally, we derive the following specification for the

start
construct from Actris’s rule
5
and Iris’s rule
4
:

\frac{\forall c_{2}.\thinspace{\left\{c_{2}\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathit{prot}}\right\}}\thinspace\mathit{f}\;c_{2}\thinspace{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}}{{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{start}\ {\mathit{f}}\thinspace{\left\{c_{1}.\thinspace c_{1}\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-start}}

4.3.
Adequacy of Actris

By virtue of being an extension of Iris, Actris inherits Iris’s adequacy
theorem (
Section

3.4
), which says that a closed proof of a Hoare
triple gives rise to safety (programs cannot go wrong) and postcondition validity.
In Actris this means that the implementation of the message passing operations
(
Section

2.4
) cannot go wrong, and that transferred
messages cannot cause the program to go wrong down the line.

Many conventional session-type systems additionally ensure deadlock freedom—which means
that program execution cannot result in a state where all threads are waiting
on a message to be sent.
Deadlock freedom is ensured through a linear type system and combining
thread and channel creation into a
start
primitive.
Actris is affine (instead of linear), has a
fork
and

new_chan
primitive (instead of a
start
primitive), and supports
