locks for channel sharing.
Actris thus provides more flexibility in terms of what programs can be written
and verified
(there exist programs that are deadlock free, but cannot be type-checked using
conventional session types, while they can be verified using Actris).
On the flip side, using Actris one can prove Hoare triples for programs that
deadlock, for example:

{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace(c,c^{\prime}):\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_chan}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}

Indeed, in our operational semantics programs such as the above are safe.
The semantics of both lock acquisition (
acquire
) and message reception
(
recv
) is that the thread
loops until it succeeds.
Loops are considered safe in Iris (and thus also Actris), as the threads in question
will continue to take steps, although they will never terminate.

5.
A tour of Actris

This section demonstrates the core features of Actris.
We introduce and iteratively extend a simple channel-based merge sort algorithm to
demonstrate the main features of Actris (
Section

5.1
–
Section

5.6
).
Note that as the point of the sorting algorithms is to showcase the features
of Actris, they are intentionally kept simple and no effort has been made
to make them efficient (
e.g.,
to avoid spawning threads for small jobs).

5.1.
Basic protocols

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\qquad}}\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ :=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace|l|\leq 1\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace\texttt{send}\ c\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\mathtt{lsplit}\ l\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c_{1}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c_{2}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{send}\ c_{1}\ l;\ \texttt{send}\ c_{2}\ l^{\prime};\\
\quad\texttt{recv}\ c_{1};\ \texttt{recv}\ c_{2};\\
\quad\mathtt{lmerge}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l\ l^{\prime};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ ()\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l}\mathtt{sort\_client}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\\
\qquad\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{send}\ c\ l;\\
\quad\texttt{recv}\ c\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 6.
A channel-based merge sort algorithm
(the code for
\mathtt{lmerge}
and
\mathtt{lsplit}
is standard and thus elided).

We first prove functional
correctness of a simple channel-based merge sort algorithm, whose code is shown
in
Figure

6
.
The function
\mathtt{sort\_client}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l
takes a comparison function

\mathit{cmp}
and a linked list
l
that will be sorted.
The function mutates the linked list
l
, so it returns a unit value
()
when done.
The bulk of the work is done by the
\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c
function, which
takes a channel endpoint
c
over which it receives a linked list, and
over which it sends back
()
to inform the sender that the list has been sorted.
The function
\mathtt{sort\_service}
is implemented as follows.
If the received list is an empty or singleton list, which both are trivially sorted, the
function immediately sends back
()
.
Otherwise, the list is split into two partitions using
\mathtt{lsplit}\ l
,
which mutates the list
l
to contain the first partition, while
returning
l^{\prime}
containing the second partition.
These partitions are recursively sorted using two newly started instances of

\mathtt{sort\_service}
.
The results of the processes are then requested and merged using

\mathtt{lmerge}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l\ l^{\prime}
,
which mutates the list
l
to contain the merged list.
Finally, the unit value
()
is sent back along the original
channel endpoint
c
.

In order to verify the correctness of the sorting algorithm we first need a
specification for the comparison function
\mathit{cmp}
, which must satisfy the
following specification:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\ (\mathit{cmp}:\mathsf{Val})\triangleq\\
\quad\quad\quad(\forall x_{1}\,x_{2}.\thinspace R\ x_{1}\ x_{2}\lor R\ x_{2}\ x_{1})\ast\\
\quad\quad\quad(\forall x_{1}\,x_{2}\,v_{1}\,v_{2}.\thinspace{\left\{I\ x_{1}\ v_{1}*I\ x_{2}\ v_{2}\right\}}\thinspace\mathit{cmp}\ v_{1}\ v_{2}\thinspace{\left\{r.\thinspace r=R\ x_{1}\ x_{2}*I\ x_{1}\ v_{1}*I\ x_{2}\ v_{2}\right\}})\end{array}

This definition is polymorphic in type
T
.
Here,
R
is a total relation in type
T
,
and
I
is an interpretation predicate that relates language
values to elements of type
T
.
While the relation
R
dictates the ordering, the interpretation predicate

I
allows for flexibility about what is ordered.
Setting
I
to
e.g.,

\lambda x\;v.\thinspace v\mapsto x

orders references by what they point to in memory, rather than the memory address
itself.
To specify how lists are laid out in memory we use the following notation:

\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}\triangleq\begin{cases}\ell\mapsto\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inl}\;()&\textnormal{if $\vec{x}=\epsilon$}\\
\exists v_{1}\;\ell_{2}.\thinspace\ell\mapsto\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inr}\;(v_{1},\ell_{2})*I\ x_{1}\ v_{1}*\ell_{2}\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}_{2}&\textnormal{if $\vec{x}=[x_{1}]\cdot\vec{x}_{2}$}\end{cases}

The channel endpoint
c
adheres to the following dependent separation protocol:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq\\
\qquad\textnormal{{!}}\,(\vec{x}:\mathsf{List}\ T)\;(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{y}\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}*\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{y}\ \vec{x}\right\}}.\,{\mathbf{end}}}\end{array}

The protocol describes the interaction of first sending a linked list, and then
receiving a unit value
()
once the list is sorted.
The predicate
\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{y}\ \vec{x}

is true iff
\vec{y}
is a sorted version of
\vec{x}
with respect to
the relation
R
.
We prove the following specifications of the service and the client:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R}\cdot\mathit{prot}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_client}\ \mathit{cmp}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{y}\ \vec{x}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

There are two important things to note about these specifications.
First, the protocol
\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R
is written from the
point of view of the client.
As such, the precondition for
\mathtt{sort\_service}
requires that
c

follows the dual
\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R}
.
Second, the pre- and postcondition of
\mathtt{sort\_service}
are generalised
to have an arbitrary protocol
\mathit{prot}
appended at the end.
It is important to write specifications this way, so they can be embedded in
other protocols.
We will see examples of such an embedding in
Section

5.4
and
Section

5.5
.

The proof of these specifications is almost entirely performed by symbolic
execution using the rules
5
,
5
,

5
, and the standard separation logic rules.

Now that we have proven Hoare triples for
\mathtt{sort\_service}
and

\mathtt{sort\_client}
, we can use them to prove Hoare triples of other programs
that use these functions.
Recall that if we use them to prove a Hoare triple of a closed program, we
obtain safety and postcondition validity by virtue of Actris’s
adequacy theorem (
Section

3.4
).

5.2.
Transferring functions

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{func}}\ c:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace\mathit{cmp}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{func}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{func}}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{send}\ c\ \mathit{cmp};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ l;\ \texttt{recv}\ c\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 7.
A version of the sort service that receives the comparison function
over the channel.

The channel-based
\mathtt{sort\_service}
from the previous section
(
Figure

6
) is parametric on a comparison function.
To demonstrate Actris’s support for reasoning about functions transferred over
channels, we verify the correctness of the function

\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{func}}\ c
in
Figure

7
.
This function takes a channel endpoint
c
, over which it receives the comparison
function
\mathit{cmp}
(instead of via a function argument), followed by the
list to sort.
Similar to the service in
Section

5.1
, it mutates the list,
and sends back
()
when done.
To verify this program, we extend the protocol
\mathsf{sort\_prot}
from

Section

5.1
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{func}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\textnormal{{!}}(T:\mathsf{Type})\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\ (\mathit{cmp}:\mathsf{Val})

\displaystyle\quad\langle\mathit{cmp}\rangle{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}\right\}}.\,\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R

The new protocol specifies that we first send a comparison function
\mathit{cmp}
.
It includes binders for the polymorphic type
T
, the
interpretation predicate
I
, and the relation
R
.
The specifications are much the same as before, with the proofs being similar
besides the addition of a symbolic execution step to resolve the sending and
receiving of the comparison function:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{func}}}\cdot\mathit{prot}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{func}}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{func}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}*\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{y}\ \vec{x}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

5.3.
Choice

Branching communication is commonly modelled using the
choice

session types
\mathop{\&}
for branching and
\mathop{\oplus}
for selection.
We show that corresponding dependent separation protocols can readily be encoded in Actris.
At the level of the programming language, the instructions for choice are
encoded by sending and receiving a boolean value that is matched using an if-then-else construct:

\displaystyle\texttt{select}\ e\ e^{\prime}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{send}\ e\ e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ e\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\Rightarrow e_{1}\ |\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\Rightarrow e_{2}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace\texttt{recv}\ e\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace e_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace e_{2}

The instructions are syntactic sugar,
i.e.,
defined in the meta language (using
\triangleq
), which effectively
means that the arguments are evaluated lazily.
We define syntactic sugar
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\triangleq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} true}
and
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\triangleq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}
to be used
together with
select
for readability’s sake.

Due to the higher-order nature of Actris, the usual protocol specifications for
choice from session types can be encoded as regular logical branching within
the protocols:

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{1}\ {}_{\{Q_{1}\}}{\mathop{\oplus}}_{\{Q_{2}\}}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\textnormal{{!}}\,(b:\mathbb{B})\,\langle b\rangle{\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace b\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace Q_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace Q_{2}\right\}}.\,{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace b\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{2}}

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{1}\ {}_{\{Q_{1}\}}{\mathop{\&}}_{\{Q_{2}\}}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(b:\mathbb{B})\,\langle b\rangle{\left\{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace b\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace Q_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace Q_{2}\right\}}.\,{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace b\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{2}}

We often omit the conditions
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, which simply means
that they are
True
.
The following rules can be directly derived from the rules

5
and
5
:

\frac{}{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{1}\!\ {}_{\{Q_{1}\}}{\mathop{\oplus}}_{\{Q_{2}\}}\ \!\mathit{prot}_{2}\ *\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\ b\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\ Q_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\ Q_{2}\end{array}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{select}\ c\ b\thinspace{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\ b\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-select}}\frac{{\left\{P*Q_{1}*c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{1}\right\}}\thinspace e_{1}\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace R\right\}}{\left\{P*Q_{2}*c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{2}\right\}}\thinspace e_{2}\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace R\right\}}}{{\left\{P*c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{1}\ {}_{\{Q_{1}\}}{\mathop{\&}}_{\{Q_{2}\}}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\Rightarrow e_{1}\ |\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\Rightarrow e_{2}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace R\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Ht-branch}}

Apart from branching on boolean values, dependent separation protocols can be used to encode
choice on any enumeration type (
e.g.,
lists, natural numbers, days of the
week,
etc.
).
These encodings follow the same scheme.

5.4.
Recursive protocols

\begin{array}[]{l l}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c:=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;} l@{}}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c;\\
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\end{array}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&()\\
\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\\
\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\mathtt{liter}\ (\lambda l^{\prime}.\thinspace\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ l^{\prime};\ \texttt{recv}\ c)\ l;\\
\quad\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 8.
A recursive version of the sort service that can perform multiple jobs in sequence
(the code for the function
\mathtt{liter}
, which applies a function to each
element of the list, is standard and has been elided).

We now use choice and recursion to verify the correctness
of a sorting service that supports performing multiple sorting jobs in sequence.
The code of the sorting service
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}

and a possible
client
\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{rec}}
are displayed
in
Figure

8
.
The service
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c
takes a comparison
function
\mathit{cmp}
and a channel endpoint
c
, and returns
()
.
It contains a loop in which choice is used to either terminate the service,
or to sort an individual list using the
channel-based merge sort algorithm
\mathtt{sort\_service}

from
Section

5.1
.
The client
\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l
takes a comparison
function
\mathit{cmp}
and a nested linked list of linked lists
l
,
and returns
()
.
It starts a single instance of the service at channel endpoint
c
,
and then sequentially sends requests to sort each inner linked list
l^{\prime}

in
l
.
Finally, the client selects the terminating branch to end the communication with
the service.
A protocol for interacting with the sorting service can be defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{rec}}\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace(\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R\cdot\mathit{rec})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}\end{array}

The protocol uses the choice operator
\mathop{\oplus}
to specify that the client
may either request the service to perform a sorting job, or terminate
communication with the service.
After the job has been finished the protocol proceeds
recursively.

We use Iris’s operator
\mu x:\tau.\thinspace t
for guarded recursion
(
Section

3.3
) to define recursive protocols.
It is important to recall that—as is usual in logics with guarded
recursion—the variable
x
should appear under a

contractive
term construct in the body
t
of
\mu x:\tau.\thinspace t
.
In our protocol, the recursive variable
\mathit{rec}
appears under the argument of

\mathop{\oplus}
, which is defined in terms of
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
,
which, similarly to
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
, is contractive in the
tail protocol
\mathit{prot}
.
We can then prove the following specifications of the service and the client:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{} l}\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}\ *\\
c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{rec}}\ I\ R}\cdot\mathit{prot}\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{J}\vec{\vec{x}}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{rec}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{\vec{y}}.\thinspace|\vec{\vec{y}}|=|\vec{\vec{x}}|*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{J}\vec{\vec{y}}*(\forall i<|\vec{\vec{x}}|.\thinspace\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{\vec{y}}_{i}\ \vec{\vec{x}}_{i})\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

We let
J\triangleq\lambda\ell^{\prime}\,\vec{y}.\thinspace\ell^{\prime}\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}

to express that
\ell
points to a list of lists
\vec{\vec{x}}
.
The proof of the service follows naturally by symbolic execution
using the induction hypothesis (obtained from
4
), the
rules
5.3
and
5.3
, and the specification
of
\mathtt{sort\_service}
.
Note that we rely on the specification of
\mathtt{sort\_service}
having an
arbitrary protocol as its suffix.

It is worth pointing out that protocols in Actris provide a lot of flexibility.
Using just minor changes, we can extend the protocol to support transferring a
comparison function over the channel, like the extension made in

\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{func}}
,
or in a way such that a different comparison function can be used for each sorting job.

5.5.
Higher-order protocols

Higher-order communication is a common feature within communication protocols,
and particularly the session-types community—it is the concept of transferring
a channel endpoint over a channel, often called delegation.
Due to the impredicativity of dependent separation protocols in Actris, higher-order reasoning about
programs with delegation is readily available.
The protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
can simply refer to the channel endpoint
ownership
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}^{\prime}
in the proposition
P
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ :=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\\
\quad\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ c^{\prime};\\
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\end{array}}\hfil\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&()\\
\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace k\mathrel{:=}\mathtt{lnil}\ ()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\begin{array}[]{@{}l @{} l @{}}\mathtt{liter}\ (\lambda l^{\prime}.&\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\\
&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
&\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ l^{\prime};\ \mathtt{lcons}\ c^{\prime}\ k)\ l\end{array}\\
\quad\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right};\\
\quad\mathtt{liter}\ \texttt{recv}\ k\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 9.
A recursive version of the sort service that uses delegation to perform
multiple jobs in parallel (the code for the function
\mathtt{lcons}
,
which pushes an element to the head of a list, has been elided).

An example of a program that uses delegation is the
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}

variant of the recursive sorting service in
Figure

9
, which allows
multiple sorting jobs to be performed in parallel.
The function
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c
takes a comparison
function
\mathit{cmp}
, a channel endpoint
c
, and returns
()
.
Using the channel endpoint
c
, a client can request the service to start a new inner
sorting service
c^{\prime}
, which the service delegates over channel endpoint
c
.

Similar to the client in
Section

5.4
,
the client
\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l
takes a comparison
function
\mathit{cmp}
and a nested linked list of linked lists
l
,
and returns
()
.
The client starts a connection
c
to the service, and for each inner
list
l^{\prime}
, it acquires a delegated channel endpoint
c^{\prime}
, over which it
sends the inner list
l^{\prime}
that should be sorted.
The client keeps track of all channels to delegated services in a linked
list
k
so that it can wait for all of them to finish (using

\mathtt{liter}\ \texttt{recv}
).

A protocol for the delegation service can be defined as follows,
denoting that the client can select whether to acquire a
connection to a new delegated service or to terminate:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{del}}\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq\\
\qquad\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(c:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle c\rangle{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathsf{sort\_prot}\ I\ R\right\}}.\,\mathit{rec})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}\end{array}

We can then prove the following specifications of the service and the client:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{} l}\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}\ *\\
c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{del}}\ I\ R}\cdot\mathit{prot}\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{J}\vec{\vec{x}}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{del}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{\vec{y}}.\thinspace|\vec{\vec{y}}|=|\vec{\vec{x}}|*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{J}\vec{\vec{y}}*(\forall i<|\vec{\vec{x}}|.\thinspace\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{\vec{y}}_{i}\ \vec{\vec{x}}_{i})\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

As before, we let
J\triangleq\lambda\ell^{\prime}\,\vec{y}.\thinspace\ell^{\prime}\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}

to express that
\ell
points to a list of lists
\vec{\vec{x}}
.
Once again the proofs are straightforward, as they are simply a combination of
recursive reasoning combined with the application of Actris’s rules for channels.

5.6.
Dependent protocols

The protocols we have seen so far have only made limited use of Actris’s support
for recursion.
We now demonstrate Actris’s support for dependent protocols, which make it
possible to keep track of the history of what messages have been sent and
received.
We demonstrate this feature by considering a fine-grained version of the
channel-based merge-sort service as shown in
Figure

10
.
Like previous versions, the function
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c

takes a comparison function
\mathit{cmp}
and a channel endpoint
c
, and returns
()
.
However, unlike previous versions, the input list should be transferred
element by element over the channel endpoint
c
to the service,
and when done, the service sends back the
sorted list element by element.
We use choice to indicate whether the whole list has been
sent (
right
) or another element remains to be sent (
left
).

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ :=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x_{1}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ x_{1};\\
\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\end{array}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x_{2}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c_{1}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c_{2}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{select}\ c_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c_{1}\ x_{1};\\
\texttt{select}\ c_{2}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c_{2}\ x_{2};\\
\mathtt{split}_{\mathit{fg}}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2};\ \mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2}\end{array}}\hfil\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\end{array}}\hfil\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\\
\\[-6.99997pt]
\mathtt{split}_{\mathit{fg}}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2}\ :=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{select}\ c_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right};\\
\texttt{select}\ c_{2}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}\end{array}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{select}\ c_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c_{1}\ x;\\
\mathtt{split}_{\mathit{fg}}\ c\ c_{2}\ c_{1}\end{array}}\hfil\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2}:=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c_{1}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} assert}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} false}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c_{1}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ x\ c_{1}\ c_{2}\end{array}}\hfil\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\\
\\[-6.99997pt]
\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ x\ c_{1}\ c_{2}:=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c_{2}\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;}l}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\texttt{send}\ c\ x_{1};\\
\mathtt{transfer}\ c_{1}\ c\end{array}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\\
\lx@intercol\quad{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c_{2}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace{\mathit{cmp}\ x\ y}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\\
\quad\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ x;\\
\quad\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ y\ c_{2}\ c_{1}\\
\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\\
\quad\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ y;\\
\quad\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ x\ c_{1}\ c_{2}\end{array}}\hfil\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\\
\\[-6.99997pt]
\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l\ :=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\\
\qquad\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp})}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\mathtt{send\_all}\ c\ l;\ \mathtt{recv\_all}\ c\ l\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10.
A fine-grained version of the sort service that transfers elements one
by one (the code for the functions
\mathtt{transfer}
,

\mathtt{send\_all}
, and
\mathtt{recv\_all}
has been elided).

The structure of
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}

is somewhat similar to the coarse-grained merge-sort algorithm that we have
seen before.
The base cases of the empty or the singleton list are handled initially.
This is achieved by waiting for at least two values before starting
the recursive sub-services
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
In the base cases the values are sent back immediately, as they are trivially
sorted.
The inductive case is handled by starting two sub-services at the
channel endpoints
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
First, each of the channel endpoints are sent one of the two initially
received elements.
The remaining elements are then received by the parent service on
c
,
and forwarded to the sub-services alternatingly on
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
,
using the function
\mathtt{split}_{\mathit{fg}}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2}
.
Once the
right
flag is received, the
\mathtt{split}_{\mathit{fg}}

function terminates, and the algorithm moves to the second phase.

In the second phase, the function
\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ c_{1}\ c_{2}

is used to merge the stream of elements returned by the sub-services
on
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
and forwards them to the parent service on
c
.
It initially acquires the first
element
x
from the first sub-service on
c_{1}
,
which it passes to the auxiliary function

\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}
as the current largest value.
The auxiliary function

\mathtt{merge}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{aux}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ c\ x\ c_{1}\ c_{2}

recursively requests a value
y
from the sub-service from which the current
largest value was not acquired from (initially
c_{2}
).
It then compares
x
and
y
using the comparison function
\mathit{cmp}
,
and forwards the smallest element on
c
.
This is repeated until the
right
flag is received from either sub-service,
after which the remaining values of the other sub-service are forwarded to the
parent service on
c
using

\mathtt{transfer}\ c_{1}\ c
.

The interface of the client
\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ l

is similar to the one from
Sections

5.1
and
5.2
.
It takes a comparison function
\mathit{cmp}
and a linked lists
l
,
sorts the linked list
l
, and returns
()
when done.
The client sorts the list
l
by sending its elements to the sort
service using the
\mathtt{send\_all}\ c\ l
function (which mutates the
list
l
by removing all of its values and sending them over the
channel
c
),
and puts the received values back into the linked list using the

\mathtt{recv\_all}\ c\ l
function
(which also mutates the list
l
).
A suitable protocol for proving functional correctness of the fine-grained
sorting service is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}\;(I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{head}}\ I\ R\ \epsilon

\displaystyle\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{head}}\;(I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace

\displaystyle\hskip-220.00034pt\lambda\vec{x}.\thinspace(\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:T)\ (v:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{I\ x\ v\right\}}.\,{\mathit{rec}\ (\vec{x}\cdot[x])})\ \mathop{\oplus}\ \mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{tail}}\ I\ R\ \vec{x}\ \epsilon

\displaystyle\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{tail}}\ (I:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (R:T\to T\to\mathbb{B})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace

\displaystyle\hskip-220.00034pt\lambda\vec{x}\ \vec{y}.\thinspace(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(y:T)\ (v:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{(\forall i<|\vec{y}|.\thinspace R\ \vec{y}_{i}\ y)*I\ y\ v\right\}}.\,{\mathit{rec}\ \vec{x}\ (\vec{y}\cdot[y])})\ \ {\mathop{\&}}_{\{\vec{x}\equiv_{\mathsf{p}}\vec{y}\}}\ \ \mathbf{end}

The protocol is split into two phases
\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{head}}
and

\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{tail}}
, mimicking the behaviour of the program.
The
\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{head}}
phase is indexed by the values
\vec{x}
that
have been sent so far.
The protocol describes that one can either send another value and proceed
recursively, or stop, which moves the protocol to the next phase.

The
\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}^{\mathit{tail}}
phase is dependent on the list of values
\vec{x}

received in the first phase, and the list of values
\vec{y}
returned so
far.
The condition
(\forall i<|\vec{y}|.\thinspace R\ \vec{y}_{i}\ y)

states that the received element is larger than any of the elements that have
previously been returned,
which maintains the invariant that the stream of received elements is sorted.
When the
right
flag is received
\vec{x}\equiv_{\mathsf{p}}\vec{y}
shows that the
received values
\vec{y}
are a permutation of the ones
\vec{x}
that were sent,
making sure that all of the sent elements have been accounted for.

We can then prove top-level specifications for the service and client that are
similar to the coarse-grained version of the channel-based merge sort:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}\ I\ R}\cdot\mathit{prot}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathsf{sort\_prot}_{\mathit{fg}}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{cmp\_spec}\ I\ R\ \mathit{cmp}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{x}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{sort\_client}_{\mathit{fg}}\ \mathit{cmp}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I}\vec{y}*\mathsf{sorted\_of}_{R}\ \vec{y}\ \vec{x}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

Proving these specifications requires one to pick appropriate specifications for
the auxiliary functions to capture the required invariants with regard to
sorting.
After having picked these specifications, the parts of the proofs that involve
communication are mostly straightforward, but require a number of trivial
auxiliary results
about sorting and permutations.

6.
Subprotocols

This section describes
Actris 2.0
, which extends
Actris 1.0—as presented in the conference version of this
paper
HBK 20
—with
subprotocols
, inspired by
asynchronous subtyping of session types
MYH 09
,
MY 15
.
The intention of both of these relations is to capture
protocol-preserving changes,
that allow for some internal flexibility of how an endpoint fulfills a protocol,
while being indistinguishable by the other endpoint.
In particular, subprotocols have two key features.
First, they exploit the asynchronous semantics of channels by relaxing the
notion of duality, thereby making it possible
to prove functional correctness of a larger class of programs.
Second, they give rise to a more extensional approach to reasoning about dependent separation protocols,
as we can work up to the subprotocol relation rather than equality,
thereby providing more flexibility in the design and reuse of protocols.

We first introduce Actris 2.0’s subprotocol relation and its proof rules
(
Section

6.1
).
These should (similar to the Actris 1.0 logic, presented in
Section

4
)
be considered to be primitives of Actris;
in
Section

9.2
we define and prove them in Iris.
We then show how subprotocols can be employed to prove a mapper service,
which handles requests one at a time, while its client may send
multiple requests up front (
Section

6.2
).
Next, we demonstrate how the subprotocol relation allows for the composition of
slightly differing protocols, by composing a list reversal service whose protocol
is based on a list predicate that does not carry ownership, with a client whose
protocol is based on a list predicate that does carry ownership
(
Section

6.3
).
Finally, we show that the subprotocol relation is coinductive, and, when
combined with
4
induction, can be used to reason about recursive
protocols (
Section

6.4
).

6.1.
The subprotocol relation

The dependent separation protocols of channel endpoints are picked on channel creation (using the rule

5
shown in
Figure

5
),
which then determines how the channel endpoints should interact.
To ensure safe communication, Actris adapts the notion of duality from session
types, which requires every send (
!
) of one endpoint to be paired with a receive
(
?
) for the other endpoint, and
vice versa
.
However, working with a channel’s protocol and its dual is more restrictive than
strictly necessary.
Some variations from the original protocol preserve the
externally observed interaction, as the other endpoint is
agnostic to the variations in question, which will be made clear momentarily.
We capture some of these so-called protocol-preserving variations via a new
notion—the

subprotocol relation
—denoted as follows:

\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}

The subprotocol relation describes that protocol
\mathit{prot}_{1}
is
stronger
than
\mathit{prot}_{2}
,
or conversely, that protocol
\mathit{prot}_{2}
is
weaker
than
\mathit{prot}_{1}
.
More specifically, this means that
\mathit{prot}_{2}
can be used
in place of

\mathit{prot}_{1}
whenever such a protocol is expected during verification.
This property is captured by the following monotonicity rule for channel
ownership:

\frac{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{1}{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}}}{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{2}}

The subprotocol relation is inspired by asynchronous subtyping for session types

MYH 09
,
MY 15
, which allows (1) sending subtypes (contravariance),
(2) receiving supertypes (covariance), and (3) swapping sends ahead of receives.
These variations preserve the protocol, as (1) the originally expected type that
is to be sent can be derived
from the subtype, (2) the originally expected type to be received can be derived
from the supertype, and (3) sends do not block because channels are buffered in both directions, so messages can be enqueued ahead of time.
These variations, including the swapping property,
are generalised to dependent separation protocols using the following proof rules:

\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P_{2}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}P_{1}\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}}{\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P_{1}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P_{2}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-send-mono'}}\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P_{1}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}P_{2}\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P_{1}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P_{2}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-recv-mono'}}\frac{}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-swap'}}

The rules
6.1
and
6.1

use
separation implication

P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}Q
—which states that
ownership of
Q
can be obtained by giving up ownership
of
P
—to mimic the contra- and covariance of session subtyping.
The rule
6.1
states that sends can be swapped ahead of receives.
To be well-formed, this rule has the implicit side condition that
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
does not
bind into
w
and
Q
, and that
\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}
does not bind into
v

and
P
.

To give an intuition behind the protocol-consistent changes that the above rules
capture, consider the following subprotocol derivation:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\ } l @{\qquad} l @{}}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(i:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle i\rangle{\left\{i<42\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(j:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle j\rangle{\left\{j>42\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\textmd{{{\ref{SP-send-mono'}}}}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(i:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle i\rangle{\left\{i<42\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(j:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle j\rangle{\left\{j>50\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\textmd{{{\ref{SP-recv-mono'}}}}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(i:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle i\rangle{\left\{i<40\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(j:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle j\rangle{\left\{j>50\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\textmd{{{\ref{SP-swap'}}}}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,(j:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle j\rangle{\left\{j>50\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(i:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle i\rangle{\left\{i<40\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\end{array}

Here, we first strengthen the proposition of the send (by increasing the bound
from
j>42
to
j>50
), then weaken the proposition of the receive
(by reducing the bound from
i<42
to
i<40
),
and finally swap the send ahead of the receive.

While the aforementioned rules cover the intuition behind Actris’s subprotocol
relation, Actris’s actual subprotocol rules provide a number of additional
features:

Grammar:

\displaystyle t,u,P,Q,\mathit{prot}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\ldots\mid\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\mid\ldots

Logical variable manipulation and resource transfer:

\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\big(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}\big)}{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-send-out},
right={$\mathit{prot}_{1}\neq\mathbf{end}$}}\frac{{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]}{\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\rangle.\,{{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-send-in}}\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\big(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\big)}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-recv-out},
right={$\mathit{prot}_{2}\neq\mathbf{end}$}}\frac{{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\rangle.\,{{\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-recv-in}}

Monotonicity and swapping:

\frac{\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})}{\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-send-mono}}\quad\frac{\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-recv-mono}}\quad\frac{}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle w\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle w\rangle.\,{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-swap}}

Reflexivity and transitivity:

\frac{}{\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-refl}}\frac{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\mathit{prot}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{3}}{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{3}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-trans}}

Dual and append:

\frac{\mathit{prot}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{1}}{\overline{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\overline{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-dual}}\frac{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\mathit{prot}_{3}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{4}}{\mathit{prot}_{1}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{3}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{4}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-append}}

Channel ownership:

\frac{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{1}{\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}}}{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={$\sqsubseteq$-chan-mono}}

Figure 11.
The grammar and primitive rules of Actris 2.0 for subprotocols.

(1)

They can be used to manipulate the logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
that appear in protocols.

(2)

They can be used to transfer ownership of resources in and out of messages.

(3)

They can be used to reason about recursive protocols defined using

4
induction.

The full set of primitive rules for subprotocols is shown in
Figure

11
.
The first four rules account for logical variable manipulation and resource transfer:
Rules
11
and
11

generalise over the logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
and transfer ownership of

P
out of the weaker sending protocol
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
,
and stronger receiving protocol
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
,
respectively.
Rule
11
weakens a sending protocol

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
by instantiating the
logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
and transferring ownership of
{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
into the protocol.
Dually, the rule
11
strengthens a receiving protocol

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
by instantiating the
logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
and transferring ownership of
{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
into the protocol.

To demonstrate the intuition behind these rules consider the following proof of
the subprotocol relation presented in
Section

1.3
,
where we transfer ownership of
\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20
into a protocol,
while instantiating the logical variable
\ell_{1}
with
\ell_{1}^{\prime}
:

\hskip-55.00008pt\begin{array}[b]{@{} l @{\ } c @{\ } l @{} l @{}}\hline\cr\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20\ast\ell_{2}\mapsto 22\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20\ast\ell_{2}\mapsto 22&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-intro}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20\ast\ell_{2}\mapsto 22\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\[-3.00003pt]
\quad\quad\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\!:\!\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle({\ell_{1}},\ell_{2})\rangle{\left\{{\ell_{1}\!\mapsto\!20*\ell_{2}\!\mapsto\!22}\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\end{array}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2})\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-elim}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\[-3.00003pt]
\quad\quad\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\!:\!\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\rangle{\left\{\ell_{1}\!\mapsto\!20*\ell_{2}\!\mapsto\!22\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\end{array}&\sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
\quad\quad\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{2}\!:\!\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2})\rangle{\left\{{\ell_{2}\!\mapsto\!22}\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\end{array}

We first use rule
11
to
generalise over the logical variable
\ell_{2}
and transfer
ownership of
\ell_{2}\mapsto 22
out of the weaker protocol (
i.e.,
the send on the
RHS), and then use
11
to instantiate the logical variables
\ell_{1}^{\prime}

and
\ell_{2}
and transfer ownership of
\ell_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto 20
and
\ell_{2}\mapsto 22

into the stronger protocol (
i.e.,
the send on the LHS).

The rules for monotonicity (
11
and
11
)
and swapping (
11
) in
Figure

11
differ in two aspects
from the rules for monotonicity (
6.1
and
6.1
)
and swapping (
6.1
) that we have seen in the beginning of this
section.
First, the actual rules only apply to protocols whose head does not have
logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
and resources
P
,
i.e.,
protocols of the shape

\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}
or
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}
, instead of
those of the shape
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
or

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
.
While this restriction might seem to make the rules more restrictive, the more general rules
for monotonicity (
6.1
and
6.1
)
and swapping (
6.1
) are derivable from these simpler rules.
This is done using the rules for logical variable manipulation and resource
transfer.
Second, the actual rules for monotonicity have a later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) in
their premise.
The later modality makes these rules stronger (by
4

we have that
P
entails
\mathop{{\triangleright}}P
), and thereby internalizes its
coinductive nature into the Actris logic so
4
induction can be used to
prove subprotocol relations for recursive protocols (
Section

6.4
).

The remaining rules in
Figure

11

express that the subprotocol relation is
reflexive (
11
) and transitive (
11
),
as well as that the dual operation is anti-monotone (
11
) and
the append operation is monotone (
11
).

Let us consider the following subprotocol relation to provide some further insight
into the expressivity of our rules, (where logical variables are omitted for simplicity):

\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{{P\ast R}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}

Here we extend the protocol

\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}

with a
frame

R
.
The proposition
R
describes resources that can be sent along with the
originally
expected resources
P
, and which are reacquired along with the resources

Q
that are sent back.
We demonstrate the usefulness of this notion of framing at the protocol level
in
Section

6.3
.

The above subprotocol relation mimics the frame rule of separation logic
(
4
), which makes it possible to apply specifications while
maintaining a
frame
of resources
R
:

\frac{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q\right\}}}{{\left\{P*R\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace Q*R\right\}}}

The frame-like subprotocol relation is proven as follows:

\hskip-100.00015pt\begin{array}[]{@{} r @{\ } r @{\ } c @{\ } l @{} l @{}}&&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-recv-intro}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr Q\ast R\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-recv-elim}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr R\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-mono}}}}, {{{\ref{Later-intro}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr R\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-intro}}}}, {{{\ref{SP-trans}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr P\ast R\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-elim}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}&\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\ast R\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{Q\ast R\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}\end{array}

We use rule
11
to transfer
P
and the frame
R
out of the
weaker protocol (
i.e.,
the send on the RHS), and then use rule
11

to transfer
P
into the stronger protocol (
i.e.,
the send on the LHS), leaving us
with a context in which we still own the frame
R
.
We then use rule
11
to proceed with the receiving
part of the protocol in a dual fashion—we use rule

11
to transfer out
Q
of the stronger protocol (
i.e.,
the
receive on the LHS), and use rule
11
to transfer
Q
and the frame
R

into the weaker protocol (
i.e.,
the receive on the RHS).

6.2.
Swapping

\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{mapper\_service}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ c:=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;} l@{}}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ x\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ y;\\
\mathtt{mapper\_service}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ c\end{array}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&()\\
\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{mapper\_client}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ l\ :=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{c}\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{start}\ {(\mathtt{mapper\_service}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}})}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{n}\mathrel{:=}|l|\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\mathtt{send\_all}\ c\ l;\\
\mathtt{recvN}\ c\ l\ n;\\
\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right};\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 12.
A mapper service whose verification relies on swapping
(the code for the functions
\mathtt{send\_all}
and
\mathtt{recvN}
has been elided).

Subprotocols make it possible to verify message-passing
programs whose order of sends and receives does not match up w.r.t. duality.
As an example of such a program, let us consider the mapper service and client in

Figure

12
.
The service
\mathtt{mapper\_service}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ c
is a loop, which
iteratively receives an element over channel endpoint
c
,
maps the function
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
over that element, and sends the resulting value back.
Conversely, the client
\mathtt{mapper\_client}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ l

sends all of the elements of the list

l
up front, and only requests the mapped results back once all elements have
been sent.
Since the service interleaves the sends and receives, while the client does not,
the dependent separation protocols for the service and client cannot be dual of each other.
However, the communication between the service and client is in fact safe as
messages are buffered.
We now show that using subprotocols we can prove that this is indeed the case.
We define the protocol based on the communication where sends and receives are
interleaved:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{mapper\_prot}\ (I_{T}:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (I_{U}:U\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (\mathit{f}:T\to U)\triangleq\\
\quad\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace(\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:T)\ (v:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x\ v\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(w:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle w\rangle{\left\{I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x)}\ w\right\}}.\,\mathit{rec})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}\end{array}\end{array}

The protocol is parameterised by representation predicates
I_{T}

and
I_{U}
that relate HeapLang values to elements of type
T

and
U
in the Iris/Actris logic, and a function
\mathit{f}:T\to U

in Iris/Actris that specifies the behaviour of the HeapLang function
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
.
The connection between
\mathit{f}
and
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
is formalised as:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{f\_spec}\ (I_{T}:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (I_{U}:U\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (\mathit{f}:T\to U)\ (\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}:\mathsf{Val})\triangleq\\
\quad\forall x\,v.\thinspace{\left\{I_{T}\ x\ v\right\}}\thinspace\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ v\thinspace{\left\{w.\thinspace I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x)}\ w\right\}}\end{array}

Since
\mathtt{mapper\_prot}
describes an interleaved sequence of transactions,

\mathtt{mapper\_service}
can be readily verified against the protocol

\overline{\mathtt{mapper\_prot}}
using just the symbolic execution rules of
Actris 1.0 as presented in
Section

4.2
.
However, to verify
\mathtt{mapper\_client}
against the protocol

\mathtt{mapper\_prot}
, we need to weaken the protocol using the rules for
subprotocols of Actris 2.0.
Given a list of
n
elements, the subprotocol relation (together with an
intermediate step) that describes this weakening is:

\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{\ } l @{} @{\qquad} l}&\mathtt{mapper\_prot}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\\
\sqsubseteq&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(x_{1}:T)\ (v_{1}:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v_{1}\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x_{1}\ v_{1}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(y_{1}:U)\,\langle y_{1}\rangle{\left\{I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x_{1})}\ y_{1}\right\}}.\,\cdots\\
\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(x_{n}:T)\ (v_{n}:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v_{n}\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x_{n}\ v_{n}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(y_{n}:U)\,\langle y_{n}\rangle{\left\{I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x_{n})}\ y_{n}\right\}}.\\
\mathtt{mapper\_prot}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\textnormal{$n$ times {{{\ref{rec-unfold}}}} and}\\
\textnormal{weaken $\mathop{\oplus}$ into $\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle$}\end{array}\\
\sqsubseteq&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(x_{1}:T)\ (v_{1}:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v_{1}\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x_{1}\ v_{1}\right\}}.\,\cdots\\
\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(x_{n}:T)\ (v_{n}:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v_{n}\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x_{n}\ v_{n}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(y_{1}:U)\,\langle y_{1}\rangle{\left\{I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x_{1})}\ y_{1}\right\}}.\,\cdots\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(y_{n}:U)\,\langle y_{n}\rangle{\left\{I_{U}\ {(\mathit{f}\ x_{n})}\ y_{n}\right\}}.\\
\mathtt{mapper\_prot}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\end{array}&\textnormal{$n$ times {{{\ref{SP-swap'}}}}}\end{array}

Both steps are proven by induction on
n
.
In the first step, we unfold the recursive protocol
n
times using

4
, and use the derived rule

(\mathit{prot}_{1}\mathop{\oplus}\mathit{prot}_{2})\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}

to weaken the choices to the left choice
left
.
Recall from
Section

5.3
that
\mathop{\oplus}

is defined in terms of the send protocol (
!
).
This allows us to prove the derived rule

(\mathit{prot}_{1}\mathop{\oplus}\mathit{prot}_{2})\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}

using
11
and
11
.
The second step involves swapping all sends ahead of the receives using the rule

6.1
.

The weakened protocol that we have obtained follows the behaviour of the client,
making its verification straightforward using Actris’s rules for symbolic execution.
Concretely, we prove the following specifications for the service and the client:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\ } c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ *c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathtt{mapper\_prot}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}}\cdot\mathit{prot}\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{mapper\_service}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ *\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{mapper\_client}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{U}}\mathtt{map}\ \mathit{f}\ \vec{x}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

6.3.
Protocol compositionality

\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{service}}\ c:=\\
\quad\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\quad\mathtt{lreverse}\ l;\ \texttt{send}\ c\ ()\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{client}}\ l\ :=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{c}\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{start}\ {\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{service}}}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{send}\ c\ l;\ \texttt{recv}\ c\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 13.
A list reversing service
(the code for the function
\mathtt{lreverse}
has been elided).

An essential feature of separation logic is the ability to compose specifications
of different libraries, so that each library can be defined and verified once
against its own specification, while being used in the context of slightly
differing specifications and proofs of other libraries.
To achieve a similar property for our dependent separation protocols we would
similarly like to be able to compose compatible protocols.

A key ingredient that enables such compositionality in traditional separation logic is
the frame rule (
4
).
In
Section

6.1
we demonstrated how subprotocols allow for
similar framing in our protocols.
In this section we give a more detailed example of such framing in our protocols
by considering the service
\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{service}}\ c
in
Figure

13
,
which receives a linked list over channel endpoint
c
, reverses it, and
sends it back over
c
.

To specify this service, we could use a protocol similar to the sorting service
in
Section

5.1
, defined in terms of the
representation predicate
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}
for
linked lists:

\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\triangleq{}\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{x}:\mathsf{List}\ T)\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{x}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}

Although it is possible to verify the service against the protocol

\overline{\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}}
, this approach is not quite satisfactory.
Unlike the sorting service, the reversal service does not access the list
elements, but only changes the structure of the list.
Hence, there is no need to keep track of the ownership of the elements through
the predicate
I_{T}
.
A self-contained and simpler protocol for this service would instead be the
following:

\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\triangleq{}\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{v}:\mathsf{List}\ \mathsf{Val})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{v}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}

Here,
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}
is a version of the list representation
predicate that does not keep track of the resources of the elements, but only
describes the structure of the list.
It is defined as:

\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\triangleq\begin{cases}\ell\mapsto\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inl}\;()&\textnormal{if $\vec{v}=\epsilon$}\\
\exists\ell_{2}.\thinspace\ell\mapsto\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inr}\;(v_{1},\ell_{2})\ast\ell_{2}\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}_{2}&\textnormal{if $\vec{v}=[v_{1}]\cdot\vec{v}_{2}$}\end{cases}

However, once we have verified the service against the simple protocol,
the proof of a client might prefer to interact with the list reversal
service through the general protocol
\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}
.
Doing so can be achieved by proving the subprotocol relation

\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}
.
To prove this subprotocol relation, we first establish the following relation
between the two versions of the list representation predicate:

\textstyle\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\ \mathrel{\ast\!\!{-}\!\!\ast}\ (\exists\vec{v}.\thinspace\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\ast\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}.I_{T}\ x\ v)

Here,
\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}
is the pairwise iterated
separation conjunction over two lists of equal length,
and
\mathrel{\ast\!\!{-}\!\!\ast}
is a bi-directional separation implication.
The above result thus states that
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}

can be split into two parts, ownership of the links of the list
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}
,
and a range of interpretation predicates
I_{T}

for each element of the list, and
vice versa
.
With this result at hand, the proof of the desired subprotocol relation is
carried out as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\ } l @{}}&\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\\
=&\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{v}:\mathsf{List}\ \mathsf{Val})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{v}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{v}:\mathsf{List}\ \mathsf{Val})(\vec{x}:\mathsf{List}\ T)\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\ast\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}.I_{T}\ x\ v\right\}}.\\
&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}(\mathsf{reverse}\ \ \vec{v})\ast\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}.I_{T}\ x\ v\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{x}:\mathsf{List}\ T)\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{x}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\\
=&\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\end{array}

We first frame the range of interpretation predicates owned by the list

\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}.I_{T}\ x\ v
,
using an approach similar to the frame example in
Section

6.1
,
and then use
6.3
to combine it with
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}
and

\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{v}
for the sending and receiving step,
to turn them into
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}
and

\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{x}
, respectively.
Note that the logical variable
\vec{v}
is changed into
\vec{x}
,
using the subprotocol rules for logical variable manipulation.
With this subprotocol relation at hand, it is possible to prove the following
specifications for the service and client:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}}\cdot\mathit{prot}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{service}}\ c}\\
&{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{list\_rev}\_{\mathtt{client}}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{x}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}\end{array}

6.4.
Subprotocols and recursion

We conclude this section by showing how subprotocol relations involving recursive
protocols can be proven using
4
induction.
Recall from
Section

5
that the principle of
4
induction
is as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{{\triangleright}}P\Rightarrow P}{P}

By letting
P
be
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
, we can
prove
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
using the induction hypothesis

\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})
.
The later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) ensures that the induction hypothesis is not
used immediately, but a monotonicity rule for
send (
11
) or receive (
11
) is applied
first.
This is done typically after unfolding the recursion operator using
4
.
The monotonicity rules
11
or
11
contain
a later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) in their premise, which makes it possible to strip off the
later of the induction hypotheses (by rule
4
for monotonicity of
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
).

Our approach for proving subprotocol relations using
4
induction
is similar to the approach of Brandt and Henglein
BH 98
for proving
subtyping relations for recursive types using coinduction.
Brand and Henglein
BH 98
however have a syntactic restriction on
proofs to ensure that the induction hypothesis is not used immediately (
i.e.,
is
used in a
contractive
fashion), while
we use the later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) of Iris to achieve that.

To demonstrate how our approach works, we prove
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
,
where:

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{1}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace(\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\cdot\mathit{rec})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace(\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\cdot\mathit{rec})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}

Here,
\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}
and
\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}
are the
protocols from
Section

6.3
, for which we have already
proven
\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}
.
The proof of
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
is as follows:

\hskip-50.00008pt\begin{array}[]{@{} r @{\ } c @{\ } r @{\ } c @{\ } l @{} l @{}}\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-append}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{1}&\sqsubseteq&\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{later-mono}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{1}&\sqsubseteq&\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{select-mono}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&(\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{1})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}&\sqsubseteq&(\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})\mathop{\oplus}\mathbf{end}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{rec-unfold}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{Loeb}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr&&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}\end{array}

The proof starts with the
4
rule, followed by unfolding the
recursive types with
4
.
We then proceed with the following derived rule for monotonicity of selection (
\mathop{\oplus}
):

\frac{\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}\wedge\mathit{prot}_{3}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{4})}{(\mathit{prot}_{1}\mathop{\oplus}\mathit{prot}_{3})\sqsubseteq(\mathit{prot}_{2}\mathop{\oplus}\mathit{prot}_{4})}\quad\textsc{lab={$\mathop{\oplus}$-mono}}

Due to the regular conjunction in the premise, the same resources can be used to
prove both branches of
\mathop{\oplus}
.
This is sound because only one branch of
\mathop{\oplus}
will be chosen.
The rule
6.4
follows from
11
as selection (
\mathop{\oplus}
)
is defined in terms of send (
!
).

We continue the main proof with monotonicity of the later modality
(
4
),
which lets us strip
off the later of the induction hypothesis
\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})
.
We then use
11
, along with

\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{list\_rev\_prot}_{I_{T}}
,
which we have proven in
Section

6.3
.
The remaining proof obligation
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
follows
from the induction hypothesis.

While the protocols in the prior examples are similar in structure, our approach
scales to protocols for which that is not the case.
For example, consider
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
, where:

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{1}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle.\,\mathit{rec}

\displaystyle\mathit{prot}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle x\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,(y:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x+2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y+2\rangle.\,\mathit{rec}

Intuitively, these protocols are related, as we can unfold the body of
\mathit{prot}_{1}

twice, the body of
\mathit{prot}_{2}
once, and swap the second receive over the first send.
The proof is as follows:

\hskip-100.00015pt\begin{array}[]{@{} r @{\ } c @{\ } r @{} l @{\ } l @{} l @{}}\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-recv-mono}}}}, {{{\ref{Later-intro}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
&&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{2}&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-mono'}}}}, {{{\ref{Later-intro}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
