&&\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{2}&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-swap'}}}}, {{{\ref{SP-trans}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
&&\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{2}&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{later-mono}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
&&\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{2}&)&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{SP-send-mono'}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\textnormal{{!}}\,x\,\langle x\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq\\[-3.00003pt]
&&\textnormal{{!}}\,x\,\langle x\rangle.\,\textnormal{{!}}\,y\,\langle y\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle x\!+\!2\rangle.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle y\!+\!2\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}_{2}&&&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{rec-unfold}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})&\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}&\smash{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\ {{{\ref{Loeb}}}}\hss}}}\\
\hline\cr&&\mathit{prot}_{1}&\ \sqsubseteq&\mathit{prot}_{2}\end{array}

After we use
6.1
for the first time, we strip
off the later of the induction hypothesis

\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})
, using
4
.
Subsequently, when we use
6.1
and
11
,
there are no more laters to strip.
We therefore instead introduce the laters using
4
before
applying the appropriate subprotocol monotonicity rule.

7.
Manifest sharing via locks

Since dependent separation protocols and the connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
for ownership of protocols
are first-class objects of the Actris logic, they can be used like any other
logical connective.
This means that protocols can be combined with any other
mechanism that Actris inherits from Iris.
In particular, they can be combined with Iris’s generic invariant and ghost
state mechanism, and can be used in combination with Iris’s
abstractions for reasoning about other concurrency connectives like locks,
barriers, lock-free data structures,
etc.

In this section we demonstrate how dependent separation protocols can be combined with lock-based
concurrency.
This combination allows us to prove functional correctness of programs that
make use of the notion of
manifest sharing

BP 17
,
BTP 19
,
where channel endpoints are shared between multiple parties.
Instead of having to extend Actris, we make use of the locks and ghost state that
Actris inherits from Iris.
We present the basic idea with a simple introductory example of sharing a channel
endpoint between two parties (
Section

7.1
).
We then consider a more challenging example of a channel-based load-balancing
mapper (
Section

7.2
).

7.1.
Locks and ghost state

As presented in
Section

2
, HeapLang includes a lock library,
with the operations
\texttt{new\_lock}\;()
,
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
, and

\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}
.
The operations satisfy the separation logic specifications shown
in
Figure

14
.

Grammar:

\displaystyle t,u,P,Q,\mathit{prot}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\ldots\mid\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ P\mid\ldots

Locks:

\displaystyle{\left\{R\right\}}\thinspace

\displaystyle\texttt{new\_lock}\;()\thinspace{\left\{\mathit{lk}.\thinspace\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R\right\}}

(
Ht-new-lock
)

\displaystyle{\left\{\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R\right\}}\thinspace

(
Ht-acquire
)

(

\displaystyle\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\

Figure 14.
The grammar and rules of locks in Iris.

The specifications for locks make use of the representation
predicate
\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R
, which expresses that a lock
\mathit{lk}
guards
the resources described by the proposition
R
.
When creating a new lock one has to give up ownership of
R
, and in turn,
obtains the representation predicate
\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ R
(
Ht-new-lock
).
The representation predicate can then be freely duplicated so it
can be shared between multiple threads (
Lock-dup
).
When entering a critical section using
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
,
a thread gets exclusive
ownership of
R
(
Ht-acquire
), which has to be given up
when releasing the lock using
\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}
(
Ht-release
).
The resources
R
that are protected by the lock are therefore invariant
in-between any of the critical sections.
The lock can only ever be acquired by one thread at a time, as

\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk}
will loop until the lock is released.
The
Ht-acquire
rule reflects this, as the exclusive resources
R

are only obtained once the function terminates,
i.e.,
when the lock is available.

\displaystyle\mathtt{prog\_lock}:={}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace c\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {\thinspace}{(\lambda c.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace\mathit{lk}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{new\_lock}\;()\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} fork}\thinspace\left\{\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ 21;\ \texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}\right\};\\
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ 21;\ \texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk})\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\end{array}}

\displaystyle\texttt{recv}\ c+\texttt{recv}\ c

A sample program that combines locks and channels to
achieve manifest sharing.

To show how locks can be used, consider the program
\mathtt{prog\_lock}
in

Figure

15
.
This program uses a lock to share a channel endpoint
between two threads, which each send the integer
21
to the main thread.
The following dependent protocol specifies the expected interaction from the
point of view of the
main thread:

\mathtt{lock\_prot}\triangleq\mu(\mathit{rec}:{\rm Nature}\rightarrow\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace\lambda n.\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace(n=0)\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace\mathbf{end}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\thinspace\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle 21\rangle.\,{\mathit{rec}\ (n-1)}

Here,
n
denotes the number of messages that should be exchanged.
In the example program,
n
is initially 2.
Since
c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ n}
is an exclusive resource, we
need a lock to share it between the threads that send
21
.
For this we will use the following lock invariant:

\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ (\exists n.\thinspace\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n*c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ n})

The natural number
n
is existentially quantified since it changes
whenever a message is exchanged.
To keep track of the number of exchanges that each thread is allowed to make
we then need to tie the number
n
to some local resource.
We achieve this by using Iris’s
ghost theory
mechanism for creating
user-defined ghost state
JSS+ 15
,
JKJ+ 18
.
In particular, we define two logical connectives
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n
and

\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
using Iris.
4
4

4

Defining a ghost theory in Iris
involves picking an appropriate
resource algebra
with which one can
define a set of abstract predicates (here
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n
and

\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
).
The details of resource algebras are beyond the scope of this paper and can be
found in Jung
et al.

JKJ+ 18
.

\displaystyle\textsf{True}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle\exists\gamma.\thinspace\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!0

(
Auth-init
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}*\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!(1+n)

(
Auth-alloc
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!(1+n)*\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n

(
Auth-dealloc
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n*\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}{}

\displaystyle n>0

(
Auth-contrib-pos
)

Figure 16.
The authoritative contribution ghost theory.

The
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n
fragment can be thought of as an authority that
keeps track of the number of ongoing contributions
n
, while each

\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
is a token that witnesses that a contribution is still in
progress.
This intuition is made precise by the rules in
Figure

16
.
The rule
Auth-init
expresses that an authority
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!0

can always be created, capturing that 0 contributions are initially in progress.
A fresh ghost identifier
\gamma
is given, which is conceptually similar to how
we obtain fresh locations for newly allocated references on the physical heap.
Using the rules
Auth-alloc
and
Auth-dealloc
, we can
allocate and deallocate
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
tokens as long as the number
n

of ongoing contributions in
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n
is updated accordingly.
The rule
Auth-contrib-pos
expresses that ownership of a token

\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
implies that the count
n
of
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n

must be positive.

Most of the rules in
Figure

16
involve Iris’s
view shift

connective
\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}
for performing ghost updates.
This is made precise by the structural rules

7.1
and
7.1
, which establish
the connection between
\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}
and Iris’s Hoare triples:

\frac{P\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}P^{\prime}{\left\{P^{\prime}\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace Q^{\prime}\right\}}\forall v.\thinspace Q^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}Q}{{\left\{P\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace Q\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={Vs-csq}}\frac{P\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}Q}{P*R\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}Q*R}\quad\textsc{lab={Vs-frame}}

With the ghost theory in place, we can now prove suitable specifications
for the program.
The specification of the top-level program is shown on the right, while the
left Hoare triple shows the auxiliary specification of both threads that send
the integer
21
:

\begin{array}[]{l @{\qquad} l}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}*\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ (\exists n.\thinspace\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n*c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ n})\right\}}\\
&\quad{\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ 21;\ \texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}}\\
&{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\end{aligned}}&{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{prog\_lock}}\\
&{\left\{v.\thinspace v=42\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}

We use rule
5
to assign protocol
\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ 2
to the
channel.
To establish the initial lock invariant, we use the rules
Auth-init

and
Auth-alloc
to create the authority
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!2
and
two
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
tokens.
The
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}
tokens play a crucial role in the proofs of the
sending threads to establish that the existentially quantified variable
n
is
positive (using
Auth-contrib-pos
).
Knowing
n>0
, these threads can establish that the protocol

\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ n}
has not terminated yet (
i.e.,
is not
\mathbf{end}
).
This is needed to use the rule
5
to prove the correctness of
sending
21
, and thereby advancing the protocol from

\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ n}
to
\overline{\mathtt{lock\_prot}\ (n-1)}
.
Subsequently, the sending threads can deallocate the token
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}

(using
Auth-dealloc
) to decrement the
n
of
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n

accordingly to restore the lock invariant.

7.2.
A channel-based load-balancing mapper

This section demonstrates a more interesting use of manifest sharing.
We show how Actris can be used to verify functional correctness
of a channel-based load-balancing mapper that maps the HeapLang function

\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
over a list.
Our channel-based mapper consists of one client that distributes the work, and a
number of workers that perform the function
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
on individual elements of
the list.
To enable communication between the client and the workers, we make use of
a single channel.
One endpoint is used by the client to distribute the work between the workers,
while the other endpoint is shared between all workers to request and
return work from the client.
The implementation of the workers
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{lk}\ c
,
which can be found in

Figure

17
, consists of a loop over three phases:

(1)

The worker notifies the client that it wants to perform work (using

\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}
), after which it is then notified (using

branch
) whether there is more work or all elements have been mapped.
If there is more work, the worker receives an element
x
that needs to
be mapped.
Otherwise, the worker will terminate.

(2)

The worker maps the function
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
on
x
.

(3)

The worker notifies the client that it wants to send back a result
(using
\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}
), and subsequently sends back the
result
y
of mapping
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
on
x
.

The first and last phases are in a critical section guarded by a lock
\mathit{lk}

since they involve interaction over a shared channel endpoint.
As the sharing behaviour is encapsulated by the worker, we omit the code of the client
for brevity’s sake.
5
5

5

The entire code is present in
the accompanied Coq development
HBK 21
.

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{lk}\ c:=\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\\
\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} branch}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} with}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l @{\;\Rightarrow\;} l@{}}\ \ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right}&\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk}\\
|\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} left}&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l !{\quad\quad} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace x\mathrel{:=}\texttt{recv}\ c\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk};&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(*\ acquire work\ *)}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace y\mathrel{:=}\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ x\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(*\ map it\ *)}\\
\texttt{acquire}\ \mathit{lk};\\
\quad\texttt{select}\ c\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} right};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ y;&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(*\ send it back\ *)}\\
\texttt{release}\ \mathit{lk};\\
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{lk}\ c\end{array}\\
\end{array}\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} end}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

\displaystyle\textsf{True}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle\exists\gamma.\thinspace\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!0\ \emptyset

(
AuthM-init
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ X\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

Figure 18.
The authoritative contribution ghost theory extended with multisets.

A protocol that describes the interaction from the client’s point of view is
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{par\_mapper\_prot}\ (I_{T}:T\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (I_{U}:U\to\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{iProp})\ (\mathit{f}:T\to\mathsf{List}\ U)\triangleq\\
\quad\mu(\mathit{rec}:{\rm Nature}\to\mathsf{MultiSet}\ T\to\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace\lambda n\ X.\\
\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} if}\thinspace n=0\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} then}\thinspace\mathbf{end}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} else}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}(\textnormal{{!}}\,(x:T)\ (v:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{I_{T}\ x\ v\right\}}.\,{\mathit{rec}\ n\ (X\uplus\{x\})})\mathop{\oplus}\mathit{rec}\ (n-1)\ X\\
\quad{}_{\{(n=1)\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}(X=\emptyset)\}}{\mathop{\&}}_{\{\textsf{True}\}}\\[3.99994pt]
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(x:T)\ (\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{x\in X*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{U}}(\mathit{f}\ x)\right\}}.\,{\mathit{rec}\ n\ (X\setminus\{x\})}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Similarly to
\mathtt{mapper\_prot}
from
Section

6.2
, the protocol is
parameterised by representation predicates
I_{T}

and
I_{U}
, and a function
\mathit{f}:T\to\mathsf{List}\ U
in
the Iris/Actris logic that will be related to
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}

through a
\mathsf{f\_spec}
specification.
Similar to the protocol
\mathtt{lock\_prot}
from
Section

7.1
, the protocol

\mathsf{par\_mapper\_prot}
is indexed by the number of remaining workers
n
.
On top of that, it carries a multiset
X
describing the values currently
being processed by all the workers.
The multiset
X
is used to make sure that the returned results are in
fact the result of mapping the function
\mathit{f}
.
The condition
(n=1)\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}(X=\emptyset)
on the branching operator (
\mathop{\&}
)
expresses that the last worker may only request more work if there are no
ongoing jobs.

To accommodate sharing of the channel endpoint between all workers using a lock
invariant, we extend the authoritative contribution ghost theory from

Section

7.1
.
We do this by adding multisets
X
and
Y
to the connectives

\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ X
and
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ Y
.
These multisets keep track of the values held by the workers.
The rules for the ghost theory extended with multisets are shown in

Figure

18
.
The rules
AuthM-init
,
AuthM-alloc
and
AuthM-dealloc

are straightforward generalisations of the ones we have seen before.
The new rules
AuthM-add
and
AuthM-remove
determine that the
multiset
Y
of
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ Y
can be updated as long as it is done in
accordance with the multiset
X
of
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ X
.
Finally, the
AuthM-contrib-agree
rule expresses that the
multiset
Y
of
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ Y
must be a subset of the
multiset
X
of
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ X
,
while the stricter rule
AuthM-contrib-agree1
asserts equality between

X
and
Y
when only one contribution remains.

We then prove the following specifications of
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}

and a possible top-level client
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_client}
that uses
n

workers to map
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
over the linked list
\ell
:

\begin{array}[]{l l @{}}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,} l @{}}\mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}*\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ \emptyset\ *\\
\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ \left(\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{} l @{}}\exists n\ X.\thinspace\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ X\ *\\
\ c\rightarrowtail\overline{\mathsf{par\_mapper\_prot}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\ n\ X}\end{array}\right)\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{lk}\ c}\\
&{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\end{aligned}}&{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,} l @{\;}}\mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{T}\ I_{U}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ *\\
0<n*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{par\_mapper\_client}\ n\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace\vec{y}\equiv_{\mathsf{p}}\mathtt{flatMap}\ \mathit{f}\ \vec{x}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{U}}\vec{y}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}

The lock invariant and specification of
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}
are similar to
those used in the simple example in
Section

7.1
.
The specification of
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_client}\ n\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \ell
simply states
that the resulting linked contains a permutation of performing the
map at the level of the logic.
To specify that, we make use of
\mathtt{flatMap}:(T\to\mathsf{List}\ U)\to(\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{List}\ U)
, whose
definition is standard.

The proof of the client involves allocating the channel with the protocol

\mathsf{par\_mapper\_prot}
, with the initial number of workers
n
.
Subsequently, we use the rules
AuthM-init
and
AuthM-alloc

to create the authority
\mathsf{auth}_{\gamma}\ \!n\ \emptyset
and
n
tokens

\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ \emptyset
, which allow us to establish the lock invariant
and to distribute the tokens among the mappers.
The proof of the mapper proceeds as usual.
After acquiring the lock, the mapper obtains ownership of the lock invariant.
Since the worker owns the token
\mathsf{contrib}_{\gamma}\ \emptyset
, it knows that
the number of remaining workers
n
is positive, which allows it to conclude
that the protocol has not terminated (
i.e.,
is not
\mathbf{end}
).
After using the rules for channels, the rules
AuthM-add
and

AuthM-remove
are used to update the authority, which is needed to
reestablish the lock invariant so the lock can be released.

8.
Case study: map-reduce

As a means of demonstrating the use of Actris for verifying more
realistic programs, we present a proof of functional correctness
of a simple channel-based load-balancing implementation of the map-reduce model
by Dean and Ghemawat
DG 04
.

Since Actris is not concerned with distributed systems over networks, we
consider a version of map-reduce that delegates the work over forked-off
threads on a single machine.
This means that we do not consider mechanics like handling the failure,
restarting, and rescheduling of nodes that a version that operates on a network
has to consider.

In order to implement and verify our map-reduce version we make use of the
implementation and verification of the fine-grained channel-based merge sort
algorithm (
Section

5.6
) and the channel-based load-balancing
mapper (
Section

7.2
).
As such, our map-reduce implementation is mostly a suitable client that glues
together communication with these services.
The purpose of this section is to give a high-level description of the
implementation.
The actual code and proofs can be found in the accompanied
Coq development
HBK 21
.

8.1.
A functional specification of map-reduce

The purpose of the map-reduce model is to transform an input set of type

\mathsf{List}\ T
into an output set of type
\mathsf{List}\ V

using two functions
\mathit{f}
(often called “map”) and
\mathit{g}
(often
called “reduce”):

\mathit{f}:T\to\mathsf{List}\ (K*U)\quad\quad\mathit{g}:(K*\mathsf{List}\ U)\to\mathsf{List}\ V

An implementation of map-reduce performs the transformation in three steps:

(1)

First, the function
\mathit{f}
is applied to each element of the input set.
This results in lists of key/value pairs which are then flattened
using a
\mathtt{flatMap}
operation (an operation that takes a list of lists and appends all nested lists):

\mathtt{flatMap}\ \mathit{f}\quad:\quad\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{List}\ (K*U)

(2)

Second, the resulting lists of key/value pairs are grouped together by
their key (this step is often called “shuffling”):

\mathtt{group}\quad:\quad\mathsf{List}\ (K*U)\to\mathsf{List}\ (K*\mathsf{List}\ U)

(3)

Finally, the grouped key/value pairs are passed on to the
\mathit{g}

function, after which the results are flattened to aggregate the results.
This is done using a
\mathtt{flatMap}
operation:

\mathtt{flatMap}\ \mathit{g}\quad:\quad\mathsf{List}\ (K*\mathsf{List}\ U)\to\mathsf{List}\ V

The complete functionality of map-reduce is equivalent to applying the following

\mathtt{map\_reduce}
function on the entire data set:

\mathtt{map\_reduce}\quad:\quad\mathsf{List}\ T\to\mathsf{List}\ V\quad\triangleq\quad(\mathtt{flatMap}\ \mathit{g})\circ\mathtt{group}\circ(\mathtt{flatMap}\ \mathit{f})

A standard instance of map-reduce is counting word occurrences, where we let

T\triangleq K\triangleq\mathsf{String}
and
U\triangleq{\rm Nature}
and

V\triangleq\mathsf{String}*{\rm Nature}
with:

\displaystyle\mathit{f}:\mathsf{String}\to\mathsf{List}\ (\mathsf{String}*{\rm Nature})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda x.\thinspace[(x,1)]

\displaystyle\mathit{g}:(\mathsf{String}*\mathsf{List}\ {\rm Nature})\to\mathsf{List}\ (\mathsf{String}*{\rm Nature})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda(k,\vec{n}).\thinspace[(k,\Sigma_{i<|\vec{n}|}.\ \vec{n}_{i})]

8.2.
Implementation of map-reduce

The general distributed model of map-reduce is achieved by delegating the
phases of mapping, shuffling, and reducing, over a number of worker nodes
(
e.g.,
nodes of a cluster or individual CPUs).
To perform the computation in a delegated way, there is some work involved
in coordinating the jobs over these worker nodes,
which is usually done as follows:

(1)

Split the input data into chunks and delegate these chunks to worker
nodes, that each apply the “map” function
\mathit{f}
to their given data in
parallel. We call these nodes the “mappers”.

(2)

Collect the complete set of mapped results and “shuffle” them,
i.e.,
group them by key.
The grouping is commonly implemented using a parallel sorting algorithm.

(3)

Split the shuffled data into chunks and delegate these chunks to worker
nodes that each apply the “reduce” function
\mathit{g}
to their given
data in parallel. We call these nodes the “reducers”.

(4)

Collect and aggregate the complete set of result of the reducers.

Our variant of the map-reduce model is defined as a function

\mathtt{map\_reduce}_{\mathsf{v}}\ n\ m\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \ell
in HeapLang, which coordinates the work
for performing map-reduce on a linked list
\ell
between
n
mappers
applying the HeapLang “map” function
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
,
and
m
reducers applying the HeapLang “reduce” function
\mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}
.
To make the implementation more interesting, we prevent storing intermediate
values locally by forwarding/returning them immediately as they are
available/requested.
The global structure is as follows:

(1)

Start
n
instances of the load-balancing

\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}
from
Section

7
,
parameterised with the
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
function, acting as the mappers.
Additionally start an instance of
\mathtt{sort\_service}_{\mathit{fg}}
from

Section

5.6
, parameterised by a concrete comparison
function on the keys, corresponding to
\lambda(k_{1},\_)\ (k_{2},\_).\thinspace k_{1}<k_{2}
.
Note that the type of keys are restricted to be integers
for brevity’s sake.

(2)

Perform a loop that handles communication with the mappers.
If a mapper requests work, pop a value from the input list.
If a mapper returns work, forward it to the sorting service.
This process is repeated until all inputs have been mapped and forwarded.

(3)

Start
m
instances of the
\mathtt{par\_mapper\_worker}
, parameterised by
\mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}
, acting as the reducers.

(4)

Perform a loop that handles communication with the mappers.
If a mapper requests work, group elements returned by the sort service.
If a mapper returns work, aggregate the returned value in a the linked
list.
Grouped elements are created by requesting and aggregating elements from the
sorter until the key changes.

The aggregated linked list then contains the fully mapped input set upon
completion.

8.3.
Functional correctness of map-reduce

The specification of the map-reduce program that we prove is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}{\begin{aligned} &{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}0<n\ *\ 0<m\ *\ \mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{T}\ I_{\mathbb{Z}*U}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ *\ \mathsf{f\_spec}\ I_{\mathbb{Z}*\mathsf{List}\ U}\ I_{V}\ \mathit{g}\ \mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}\ *\ \ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\end{array}\right\}}\\
&\quad{\mathtt{map\_reduce}_{\mathsf{v}}\ n\ m\ \mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}\ \ell}\\
&{\left\{\exists\vec{z}.\thinspace\vec{z}\equiv_{p}\ \mathtt{map\_reduce}\ \mathit{f}\ \mathit{g}\ \vec{x}*\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{V}}\vec{z}\right\}}\end{aligned}}\end{array}

The
\mathsf{f\_spec}
predicates (as introduced in
Section

6.2
)
establish a connection between the functions
\mathit{f}
and
\mathit{g}
in
Iris/Actris and the functions
\mathit{f}_{\mathsf{v}}
and
\mathit{g}_{\mathsf{v}}
in HeapLang.
These make use of the various interpretation predicates
I_{T}
,

I_{\mathbb{Z}*U}
,
I_{{\mathbb{Z}*\mathsf{List}\ U}}
, and
I_{V}
for
the types in question.
Lastly, the
\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}
predicate determines
that the input is a linked list of the initial type
T
.
The postcondition asserts that the result
\vec{z}
is a permutation of the
original linked list
\vec{x}
applied to the functional specification

\mathtt{map\_reduce}
of map-reduce from
Section

8.1
.

9.
The model of Actris

We construct a model of Actris as a shallow embedding in
the Iris framework
KJB+ 17
,
JSS+ 15
,
JKBD 16
,
JKJ+ 18
.
This means that the type
\mathsf{iProto}
of dependent separation protocols,
the subprotocol relation
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
,
and the connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
for the channel ownership,
are definitions in Iris,
and the Actris proof rules are lemmas about these definitions in Iris.

In this section we describe the relevant aspects of the model of Actris.
We model the type
\mathsf{iProto}
of dependent separation protocols as the solution of a recursive domain equation, and
describe how the operations for dual and composition are defined
(
Section

9.1
).
We then define the subprotocol relation
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}

and prove its proof rules as lemmas (
Section

9.2
).
To connect protocols to the endpoint channel buffers in the semantics
we define the
protocol consistency relation
,
which ensures that a pair of protocols is consistent with the messages in
their associated buffers (
Section

9.3
).
On top of the protocol consistency relation, we define the
Actris ghost theory
for
dependent separation protocols (
Section

9.4
),
which forms the key ingredient for defining the connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
for
channel ownership (
Section

9.5
) that links protocols to the
semantics of channels (
Section

2.4
).
We then show how adequacy follows from the embedding in Iris (
Section

9.6
).
Finally, we show how to solve the recursive domain equation for the type

\mathsf{iProto}
of dependent separation protocols (
Section

9.7
).

9.1.
The model of dependent separation protocols

To construct a model of dependent separation protocols, we first need to determine what they mean
semantically.
The challenging part involves the constructors

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
, whose (higher-order and
impredicative) logical variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
bind into the communicated value
v
, the
transferred resources
P
, and the tail protocol
\mathit{prot}
.
We model these constructors as predicates over the communicated value
and the tail protocol.
To describe the transferred resources
P
, we model these protocols as
Iris predicates (functions to
\mathsf{iProp}
).
This gives rise to the following recursive domain equation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{action}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\mathbf{send}\mid\mathbf{recv}

\displaystyle\mathsf{iProto}\cong{}

\displaystyle 1+(\mathsf{action}\times(\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{iProto}\to\mathsf{iProp}))

The left part of the sum type (the unit type
1
) indicates that
the protocol has terminated, while the right part describes a message that is
exchanged, expressed as an Iris predicate.
Since the recursive occurrence of
\mathsf{iProto}
in the predicate appears in negative
position,
we guard it using Iris’s
type-level later
(
\mathord{\blacktriangleright}
) operator (whose only
constructor is
\textsf{next}:T\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\ T
).
The exact way the solution is constructed is detailed in

Section

9.7
.
For now, we assume a solution exists, and define the dependent separation protocols constructors as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{end}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()

\displaystyle\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(\mathbf{send},\lambda w\,\mathit{prot}^{\prime}.\thinspace\exists\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace(v=w)*P*(\mathit{prot}^{\prime}=\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}))

\displaystyle\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(\mathbf{recv},\lambda w\,\mathit{prot}^{\prime}.\thinspace\exists\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace(v=w)*P*(\mathit{prot}^{\prime}=\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}))

The definitions of
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
make use of the (higher-order and
impredicative) existential quantifiers of Iris
to constrain the actual message
w
and tail
\mathit{prot}^{\prime}
so that they agree
with the message
v
and tail
\mathit{prot}
prescribed by the protocol.

Recursive protocols.

Iris’s guarded recursion operator
\mu x.\thinspace t
requires the
recursion variable
x
to appear under a
contractive
term construct in
t
.
Hence, to use Iris’s recursion operator to construct recursive protocols, it is
essential that the protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
are contractive in the tail
\mathit{prot}
.
To show why this is the case, let us first define what it means for a function

f:T\to U
to be contractive:

\forall x,y.\thinspace\mathop{{\triangleright}}(x=y)\Rightarrow f\;x=f\;y

Examples of contractive functions are the later modality

\mathop{{\triangleright}}:\mathsf{iProp}\to\mathsf{iProp}
and the constructor
\textsf{next}:T\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\ T
.
The protocols
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
are defined so that

\mathit{prot}
appears below a
next
, and hence we can prove
that they are contractive in
\mathit{prot}
.

Operations.

With these definitions at hand, the dual
\overline{(\_)}
and append

(\_\cdot\_)
operations are defined using Iris’s guarded
recursion operator (
\mu x.\thinspace t
):

\displaystyle\overline{(\_)}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu\mathit{rec}.\thinspace\lambda\mathit{prot}.\thinspace\begin{cases}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()&\textnormal{if }\mathit{prot}=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(\overline{a},\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\lambda w\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime}.\thinspace\exists\mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}.\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\varPhi\ w\ (\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime})\ \ast\\
\mathit{prot}^{\prime}=\textsf{next}\ (\mathit{rec}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}))\end{array}\end{array}&\textnormal{if }\mathit{prot}=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(a,\varPhi)\end{cases}

\displaystyle(\_\cdot\mathit{prot}_{2})\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mu\mathit{rec}.\thinspace\lambda\mathit{prot}_{1}.\thinspace\begin{cases}\mathit{prot}_{2}&\textnormal{if }\mathit{prot}_{1}=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()\\
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(a,\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\lambda w\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime}.\thinspace\exists\mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}.\\
\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\varPhi\ w\ (\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime})\ \ast\\
\mathit{prot}^{\prime}=\textsf{next}\ (\mathit{rec}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}))\end{array}\end{array}&\textnormal{if }\mathit{prot}_{1}=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace(a,\varPhi)\end{cases}

In the above definitions, we let
\overline{\mathbf{send}}\triangleq{}\mathbf{recv}

and
\overline{\mathbf{recv}}=\mathbf{send}
.

The base cases of both definitions are as expected.
In the recursive cases, we construct a new predicate, given the original
predicate
\varPhi
.
In these new predicates, we quantify over an original tail protocol
\mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}
such
that
\varPhi\ w\ (\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime})
holds, and unify the new tail
protocol
\mathit{prot}^{\prime}
with the result of the recursive call
\mathit{rec}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}
.

The equational rules for dual
\overline{(\_)}
and append
(\_\cdot\_)
from

Figure

5
are proven as lemmas in Iris using
4
induction.
This is possible as the recursive call
\mathit{rec}\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime}
appears below a

next
constructor—since the
next
constructor is contractive, we can
strip-off the later from the induction hypothesis when proving the equality for
the tail.

Difference from the conference version.

In the conference version of this paper
HBK 20
,
we described two versions of the recursive domain equation for dependent separation protocols:
an “ideal” version (as used in this paper), where
\mathsf{iProto}
appears in negative
position, and an “alternative” version, where
\mathsf{iProto}
appears in positive
position.
At that time, we were unable to construct a solution of the “ideal”
version, so we used the “alternative” version.
In
Section

9.7
we show how we are now able to solve the “ideal”
version.

In the conference version of this paper, the proposition
P

appeared under a later modality in the definitions of the protocols

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
,
making these protocols contractive in
P
.
This choice was motivated by the ability to construct recursive protocols like

\mu\mathit{rec}.\thinspace\textnormal{{!}}\,(c:\mathsf{Val})\,\langle c\rangle{\left\{c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}^{\prime}}
,
where the payload refers to the recursion variable
\mathit{rec}
.
In the current version (without the later modality) we can still construct such
protocols, because
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
is contractive in
\mathit{prot}
.
We removed the later modality because it is incompatible with the rules
11

and
11
for subprotocols.

9.2.
The model of the subprotocol relation

We now model the subprotocol relation
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
from

Section

6
.
For legibility, we present it in the style of an inference system through its
constructors, whereas it is formally defined using Iris’s
guarded recursion operator (
\mu x.\thinspace t
):

\frac{}{\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()\sqsubseteq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{} c @{}}\forall v,\mathit{prot}_{2}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\varPhi_{2}\;v\;(\textsf{next}\;\mathit{prot}_{2})\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\;} l @{}}\exists\mathit{prot}_{1}.&\varPhi_{1}\;v\;(\textsf{next}\;\mathit{prot}_{1})\ \ast\\
&\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})\end{array}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi_{1})}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi_{2})}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{@{} c @{}}\forall v,\mathit{prot}_{1}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\varPhi_{1}\;v\ (\textsf{next}\;\mathit{prot}_{1})\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\;} l @{}}\exists\mathit{prot}_{2}.&\varPhi_{2}\;v\;(\textsf{next}\;\mathit{prot}_{2})\ \ast\\
&\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})\end{array}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{recv},\varPhi_{1})}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{recv},\varPhi_{2})}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{} c @{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{} r @{\ } l @{}}\forall v_{1},v_{2},\mathit{prot}_{1},\mathit{prot}_{2}.&(\varPhi_{1}\ v_{1}\ (\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}_{1})\ast\varPhi_{2}\ v_{2}\ (\textsf{next}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}))\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
&\exists\mathit{prot}.\thinspace\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}})\ast\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v_{1}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{recv},\varPhi_{1})}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi_{2})}\end{array}

To be a well-formed guarded recursion definition, every
recursive occurrence of
\sqsubseteq
is guarded by the later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
).
Aside from later being required for well-formedness,
these laters make it possible to reason about
the subprotocol relation using
4
induction; both to prove the
subprotocol rules from
Figure

11
as lemmas, and for Actris users to
reason about recursive protocols as shown in
Section

6.4
.
The relation is defined in a syntax directed fashion (
i.e.,
there are no overlapping rules), and therefore all constructors need to be
defined so that they are closed under monotonicity and transitivity.

The first constructor states that terminating protocols (
\mathbf{end}\triangleq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{1}\thinspace()
) are related.
The other constructors concern the protocols

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}}
, which are modelled
as
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi)}
and
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{recv},\varPhi)}
, where

\varPhi:\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{iProto}\to\mathsf{iProp}

is a predicate over the communicated value and tail protocol.
While the actual constructors are somewhat intimidating because they are defined
in terms of these predicates in the model, they
essentially correspond to the following high-level versions:

\frac{\forall\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}.\thinspace P_{2}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\exists\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace(v_{1}=v_{2})*P_{1}*\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})}{\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v_{1}\rangle{\left\{P_{1}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle{\left\{P_{2}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P_{1}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\exists\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}.\thinspace(v_{1}=v_{2})*P_{2}*\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v_{1}\rangle{\left\{P_{1}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle{\left\{P_{2}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}\frac{\forall\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau},\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}.\thinspace(P_{1}*P_{2})\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\exists\mathit{prot}.\thinspace\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}})*\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v_{1}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})}{\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v_{1}\rangle{\left\{P_{1}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{y}\!:\!\vec{\sigma}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle{\left\{P_{2}\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}}

To obtain syntax directed rules, the first rule combines
11
,

11
, and
11
, and dually, the second
rule combines
11
,
11
, and

11
.
The third rule combines
11
,
11
and

11
and bakes in transitivity, instead of asserting
that
\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\mathit{prot}_{2}
are equal to
\textnormal{{!}}\,\langle v_{2}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}
and

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v_{1}\rangle.\,{\mathit{prot}}
, respectively.

The rules from the beginning of this section are defined by generalising
the high-level rules to arbitrary predicates.
For example, rule
\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi_{1})}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} inj}_{2}\thinspace{(\mathbf{send},\varPhi_{2})}

requires that for any value
v
and tail protocol
\mathit{prot}_{2}
that
are allowed by the predicate
\varPhi_{2}
, there is a stronger tail protocol
\mathit{prot}_{1}

(
i.e.,
where
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
),
so that the same value
v
and stronger tail protocol

\mathit{prot}_{1}
are allowed by the predicate
\varPhi_{1}
.

The rules in
Figure

11
on page
11

are proven as lemmas.
Those for logical variable and resource manipulation (
11
,

11
,
11
and
11
),
monotonicity (
11
and
11
),
and swapping (
11
) follow
almost immediately from the definition, whereas
those for reflexivity (
11
), transitivity (
11
),
and the dual and append operator (
11
and
11
)
are proven using
4
induction.

9.3.
Protocol consistency

To connect dependent separation protocols to the semantics of channels in
Section

9.5
, we define
the
protocol consistency relation

\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}
,
which expresses that protocols
\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\mathit{prot}_{2}
are
consistent

w.r.t. channel buffers containing values
\vec{v}_{1}
and
\vec{v}_{2}
.
The consistency relation is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\exists\mathit{prot}.\thinspace

\displaystyle\hskip-130.0002pt(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle\vec{v}_{2.1}\rangle.\,{\dots\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle\vec{v}_{2.|\vec{v}_{2}|}\rangle.\,\mathit{prot}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{1})*(\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle\vec{v}_{1.1}\rangle.\,{\dots\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle\vec{v}_{1.|\vec{v}_{1}|}\rangle.\,{\overline{\mathit{prot}}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2})

Intuitively,
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}

ensures that for all messages

\vec{v}_{1}=\vec{v}_{1.1}\dotsc\vec{v}_{1.|\vec{v}_{1}|}
in transit from the endpoint described
by
\mathit{prot}_{1}
to the endpoint described by
\mathit{prot}_{2}
, the protocol
\mathit{prot}_{2}
is
expecting to receive these message in order (and
vice versa
for
\vec{v}_{2}
),
after which the remaining protocols
\mathit{prot}
and
\overline{\mathit{prot}}
are dual.
To account for weakening we close the consistency relation under subprotocols
(by using
\sqsubseteq
instead of equality).
Closure under the subprotocol relation additionally implicitly captures ownership of the
quantifiers and resources associated with the messages
\vec{v}_{1}
and
\vec{v}_{2}
.
That is, since the subprotocol relations relate the protocol arguments

\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\mathit{prot}_{2}
with protocols that specify no quantifiers or resources.
More precisely, by the definition of the subprotocol relation
(shown in
Section

9.2
), a relation such as

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle v\rangle.\,{}{\mathit{prot}_{1}}\sqsubseteq\textnormal{{?}}\!\,(\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau})\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{2}}

is equivalent to a separation implication of the form

\textsf{True}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\exists\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}.\thinspace P\ast\mathop{{\triangleright}}\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{2}
,
where the obligation
True
is trivial, meaning that it implicitly asserts
ownership of
P
.

Finally, closure under the subprotocol relation gives that

\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}
and
\mathit{prot}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{1}^{\prime}

implies
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}^{\prime}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}
,
and ensures that the consistency relation enjoys the following rules
corresponding to creating a channel, sending a message, and receiving a message:

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \epsilon\ \epsilon\ \mathit{prot}\ \overline{\mathit{prot}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}(\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ (\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}})\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\ast{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}])\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
\qquad\mathop{{\mathop{{\triangleright}}}^{|\vec{v}_{2}|}}(\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ (\vec{v}_{1}\cdot[{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]])\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2})\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ ([w]\cdot\vec{v}_{2})\ (\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}})\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\\
\qquad\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace(w={v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}])*{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\ast\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2})\end{array}

The first rule states that dual protocols are consistent w.r.t. a pair of
empty buffers.
The second rule states that a protocol
\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}

can be advanced to
\mathit{prot}_{1}
by giving up ownership of

{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
and enqueueing the value

{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]
in the buffer
\vec{v}_{1}
.
Dually, the third rule states that given a protocol

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,{\mathit{prot}_{1}}
and a buffer that contains value

w
as its head, we learn that
w
is equal to

{v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]
, and that we can obtain ownership of

{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]
by advancing the protocol to
\mathit{prot}_{1}

and dequeuing the value
w
from the buffer.
Since the relation is symmetric,
i.e.,
if
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}

then
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}
, we obtain similar rules for
the protocol
\mathit{prot}_{2}
on the right-hand side.

The last two rules are proven by case analysis on the subprotocol relation
(
\sqsubseteq
) in the assumption.
Since the subprotocol relation (
\sqsubseteq
) is defined using guarded recursion,
we obtain a later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) for each case analysis.
To prove the first of the rules, we need to perform a number of case analyses
equal to the size of the buffer
\vec{v}_{2}
,
whereas for the second rule we need to perform just a single case analysis.
These later modalities are eliminated through the
skipN
operation in
the
send
operation, see

Section

9.5
for further discussion.

9.4.
The Actris ghost theory

To provide a general interface for making Actris’s reasoning principles
independent of HeapLang,
we employ a standard ghost theory approach in Iris to compartmentalise channel
ownership.
In
Section

9.5
we define the connective
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}

for channel endpoint ownership that links the ghost theory to the buffers of
our implementation of channels in HeapLang.

\displaystyle\textsf{True}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle\exists\gamma.\thinspace(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot})*(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot})

(
ho-ghost-alloc
)

\displaystyle(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot})*(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}^{\prime})\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}{}

\displaystyle(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime})*(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}^{\prime\prime})

(
ho-ghost-update
)

\displaystyle(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot})*(\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}^{\prime})\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}{}

\displaystyle\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}=\mathit{prot}^{\prime})

(
ho-ghost-agree
)

Figure 19.
Ghost theory for higher-order ghost variables in Iris.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\textsf{True}\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}\exists\chi.\thinspace\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \epsilon\ \epsilon\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \overline{\mathit{prot}}\end{array}

(
proto-alloc
)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ (\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot})\ast{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}\\
\qquad\left(\mathop{{\mathop{{\triangleright}}}^{|\vec{v}_{2}|}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ (\vec{v}_{1}\cdot[{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]])\ \vec{v}_{2}\right)\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ ({\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}])\end{array}

(
proto-send-l
)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ (\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot})\ast{P}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}\\
\qquad\left(\mathop{{\mathop{{\triangleright}}}^{|\vec{v}_{1}|}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ (\vec{v}_{2}\cdot[{v}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}]])\right)\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ ({\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{t}}/{\vec{x}}])\end{array}

(
proto-send-r
)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ ([w]\cdot\vec{v}_{2})\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ (\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot})\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}\\
\qquad\mathop{{\triangleright}}\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace w={v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\ast{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\ast\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ ({\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}])\end{array}

(
proto-recv-l
)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ ([w]\cdot\vec{v}_{1})\ \vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ (\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot})\mathrel{{\Rrightarrow}}\\
\qquad\mathop{{\triangleright}}\exists\vec{y}.\thinspace w={v}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\ast{P}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}]\ast\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ ({\mathit{prot}}[{\vec{y}}/{\vec{x}}])\end{array}

(
proto-recv-r
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}*\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}^{\prime}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime}

(
proto-
\sqsubseteq
-l
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}*\mathit{prot}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}^{\prime}\mathrel{-\!\!\ast}\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}^{\prime}

(
proto-
\sqsubseteq
-r
)

Figure 20.
The Actris ghost theory.

The Actris ghost theory is similar in its interface to the ghost theory for
contributions that we used in
Section

7
.
We define three new logical connectives—an authority

\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}
, and tokens

\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}_{l}
and

\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}_{r}
—and prove rules about how they can be
allocated, updated, and used.
Similar to prior ghost theories, the identifier

\chi
associates the connectives to each other.
The
\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}
connective can be thought of as
an authority that governs the global state of the buffers
\vec{v}_{1}
and
\vec{v}_{2}
.
The tokens
\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}_{l}
and

\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}_{r}
provide local views of the buffers state
in terms of the protocols
\mathit{prot}_{l}
and
\mathit{prot}_{r}
.
As we will see in
Section

9.5
, the authority can be shared
using a lock, while the tokens provide unique ownership of each endpoint.

To define the connectives of the Actris ghost theory
we use Iris’s existing ghost theory for higher-order ghost variables,
revolving around the two connectives

\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}
and
\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}^{\prime}
,
which we call the inner and outer fragments, respectively.
As before, the
\gamma
is the ghost identifier that associates the connectives.
The fragments can be thought of as two pieces of a single variable,
which can only be updated in the presence of both fragments.
As a result, we know that inner and outer fragment with the same ghost identifier

\gamma
always point to the same protocol
\mathit{prot}
.
This is made precise by the rules as shown in
Figure

19
.
In particular, higher-order ghost variables are allocated in pairs

\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}
and

\gamma\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}
for an identical protocol
\mathit{prot}

(
ho-ghost-alloc
),
and they can only be updated together (
ho-ghost-update
).
This means that they will always hold the same protocol
(
ho-ghost-agree
).
The subtle part of the higher-order ghost variables is that they involve ownership
of a protocol of type
\mathsf{iProto}
, which is defined in terms of Iris propositions

\mathsf{iProp}
.
Due to the dependency on
\mathsf{iProp}
(which is covered in detail in

Sections

9.1
and
9.7
) the rule
ho-ghost-agree
only gives the
equality between the protocols under a later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
).

With Iris’s higher-order ghost variables at hand, we can define the Actris ghost theory
connectives as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\exists\mathit{prot}_{1},\mathit{prot}_{2}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\gamma_{1}\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}_{1}\ast\gamma_{2}\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}_{2}\ \ast\\
\mathop{{\triangleright}}\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}_{l}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\exists\mathit{prot}_{l}^{\prime}.\thinspace\gamma_{1}\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}_{l}^{\prime}\ast\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{l}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{l})

\displaystyle\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}_{r}\triangleq\

\displaystyle\exists\mathit{prot}_{r}^{\prime}.\thinspace\gamma_{2}\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}_{r}^{\prime}\ast\mathop{{\triangleright}}(\mathit{prot}_{r}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{r})

Since we use two higher-order ghost variables,
our identifiers
\chi\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})

are pairs of Iris ghost identifiers.
The authority
\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}

asserts ownership of the inner fragments of the higher-order ghost
variables

\gamma_{1}\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}\mapsto_{\mathord{\bullet}}\mathit{prot}_{2}

for some protocols
\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\mathit{prot}_{2}
.
It then asserts that the buffers
\vec{v}_{1}
and
\vec{v}_{2}
are consistent with respect to
those protocols
\mathit{prot}_{1}
and
\mathit{prot}_{2}

(via
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}\ \mathit{prot}_{1}\ \mathit{prot}_{2}
).
The tokens

\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}_{l}
and

\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}_{r}
respectively
assert ownership of the outer higher-order ghost variable fragments

\gamma_{1}\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}_{l}^{\prime}
and
\gamma_{2}\mapsto_{\mathord{\circ}}\mathit{prot}_{r}^{\prime}
.
Here
\mathit{prot}_{l}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{prot}_{r}^{\prime}
are protocols that are weaker than the protocol
arguments
\mathit{prot}_{l}
and
\mathit{prot}_{r}
(via
\mathit{prot}^{\prime}_{l}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{l}
and

\mathit{prot}^{\prime}_{r}\sqsubseteq\mathit{prot}_{r}
).
The explicit weakening under the subprotocol relation may seem redundant,
as weakening is already accounted for in
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}
.
However, it allows us to weaken the
protocols of the tokens without the presence of the authority as
shown momentarily.
The later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
) makes sure that
\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}

and
\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\ \mathit{prot}
are contractive in
\mathit{prot}
.

With the definitions of the ghost theory connectives at hand,
we prove the rules of the ghost theory presented in

Figure

20
.
The rule
proto-alloc
corresponds to allocation of a buffer pair, the rules

proto-send-l
and
proto-send-r
correspond to sending a
message, and the rules
proto-recv-l
and
proto-recv-r

correspond to receiving a message.
Finally, the rules
proto-

⊑

-l
and
proto-

⊑

-r
captures
that we can weaken the protocols of the tokens without the presence of the
authority.
The rules of
Figure

20
are proven through a combination of
the rules for higher-order ghost
state from
Figure

19
, and the rules for the protocol consistency
relation
\mathsf{prot\_consistent}
from
Section

9.3
.

9.5.
The model of channel ownership

To link the physical contents of the bidirectional channel
c
to the
Actris ghost theory we define the channel ownership connective as follows:

\displaystyle c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\exists\chi,l,r,\mathit{lk}.\thinspace\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}(c=(l,r,\mathit{lk})*\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot})\ \lor\\
(c=(r,l,\mathit{lk})*\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot})\end{array}\right)\ast\\
\mathsf{is\_lock}\ \mathit{lk}\ (\exists\vec{v}_{1}\,\vec{v}_{2}.\thinspace l\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}_{1}\ast r\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}_{2}\ast\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2})\end{array}

The predicate states that the referenced channel endpoint
c

is either the left
(l,r,\mathit{lk})
or the right
(r,l,\mathit{lk})
side of a channel,
and that we have exclusive ownership of the ghost token

\mathsf{prot\_own_{l}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}
or
\mathsf{prot\_own_{r}}\ \chi\ \mathit{prot}
for the
corresponding side.
Iris’s lock representation predicate
\mathsf{is\_lock}

(previously presented in
Section

7
) is used to make sharing of the
buffers possible.
The lock invariant is governed by lock
\mathit{lk}
, and carries
the ownership
l\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}_{1}

and
r\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}_{2}
of the mutable linked lists containing the channel
buffers, as well as
\mathsf{prot\_ctx}\ \chi\ \vec{v}_{1}\ \vec{v}_{2}
, which
asserts protocol consistency of the buffers with respect to the protocols.

With the definition of the channel endpoint ownership along with the ghost theory and
lock rules we then prove the channel rules
5
,

5
and
5
from
Figure

5
.
The proofs are carried out through symbolic execution to the point
where the critical section is entered, after which the rules of the Actris ghost theory
(
Figure

20
) are used to allocate or update the ghost state appropriately so that it matches
the physical channel buffers.

The need for skip instructions.

The rules
proto-send-l
and
proto-send-r
from

Figure

20
contain a number of later modalities (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
)
proportional to the other endpoint’s buffer.
As explained in
Section

9.3
these later modalities are the consequence
of having to perform a number of case analyses on the subprotocol relation,
which is defined using guarded recursion, and thus contains a later modality
for each recursive unfolding.

To eliminate these later modalities, we instrument the code of the
send
function with the
\texttt{skipN}\ (\mathtt{llength}\ r)
instruction, which performs a number
of skips equal to the size of the other endpoint’s buffer
r
.
The
skipN
instruction has the following specification:

\textstyle{\left\{\mathop{{\triangleright}}^{n}P\right\}}\thinspace\texttt{skipN}\ n\thinspace{\left\{P\right\}}

Instrumentation with skip instructions is used often in work on step-indexing,
see
e.g.,

SSB 16
,
GST+ 20
.
Instrumentation is needed because current step-indexed logics like Iris
unify physical/program steps and logical steps,
i.e.,
for
each physical/program step at most one later can be eliminated from the hypotheses.
In recent work by
Svendsen
et al.

SSB 16
,
Matsushita and Jourdan
MJ 20
, and Spies
et al.

SGG+ 21

more liberal versions of step-indexing have been proposed, but none of these
versions of step-indexing have been integrated into the main Coq development of Iris and HeapLang.

9.6.
Adequacy of Actris

Having constructed the model of Actris in Iris,
we obtain the following main result,
as first presented in
Section

3.4
:

Theorem 2

(Adequacy of Actris)
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{Val}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be a meta-level (i.e., Coq) predicate over values and
suppose
{\left\{\textsf{True}\right\}}\thinspace e\thinspace{\left\{v.\thinspace\varphi\ v\right\}}
is
derivable in Iris, then

\mathsf{safe}\ e
and

\mathsf{post\_valid}\ (e,\varphi)
.

Since Actris is an internal logic embedded in Iris, the proof is an immediate
consequence of Iris’s adequacy theorem
(
1
).

9.7.
Solving the recursive domain equation for protocols

Recall the recursive domain equation for dependent separation protocols from
Section

9.1
:

\mathsf{iProto}\cong{}1+(\mathsf{action}\times(\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{iProto}\to\mathsf{iProp}))

This recursive domain equation shows that
\mathsf{iProto}
depends on the type
\mathsf{iProp}

of Iris propositions.
To use types that depend on
\mathsf{iProp}
as part of higher-order ghost state in Iris,
such types need to be bi-functorial in
\mathsf{iProp}
.
Hence, this means that to construct
\mathsf{iProto}
, in a way that it can be used
in combination with the higher-order ghost variables in
Figure

19
,
we need to solve the following recursive domain equation:

\mathsf{iProto}(X^{-},X^{+})\cong{}1+(\mathsf{action}\times(\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{iProto}(X^{+},X^{-})\to X^{+}))

Since the recursive occurrence of
\mathsf{iProto}
appears in negative position, the
polarity needs to be inverted for
\mathsf{iProto}
to be bi-functorial.

The version of Iris’s recursive domain equation solver based
on
AR 89
,
BST 10
as mechanised in Iris’s Coq
development is not readily able to construct a solution of
\mathsf{iProto}(X^{-},X^{+})
.
Concretely, the solver can only construct solutions of non-parameterised
recursive domain equations.
While a general construction for solving such recursive domain
equations exists
[
BMSS 12
, § 7]
, that construction has not been
mechanised in Coq.
We circumvent this shortcoming by solving the following recursive domain
equation instead, in which we unfold the recursion once by hand:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{iProto}_{2}(X^{-},X^{+})\cong\\
\quad\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}1+\Big(\mathsf{action}\times\big(\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}(1+(\mathsf{action}\times(\mathsf{Val}\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{iProto}_{2}(X^{-},X^{+})\to X^{-})))\to X^{+}\big)\Big)\end{array}\end{array}

Here, the polarity in the recursive occurrence is fixed, allowing us to solve

\mathsf{iProto}_{2}(X^{-},X^{+})
using Iris’s existing recursive domain equation solver.
This is sufficient because a solution of
\mathsf{iProto}_{2}(X^{-},X^{+})
is isomorphic
to a solution of
\mathsf{iProto}(X^{-},X^{+})
.

10.
Coq mechanisation

The definition of the Actris logic, its model, and the proofs of all examples
in this paper have been fully mechanised using the Coq proof
assistant
Coq 20
.
In this section we will elaborate on the mechanisation effort
(
Section

10.1
), and go through the full proof of a message-passing program
(
Section

10.2
) and a subprotocol relation (
Section

10.3
)
showcasing the tactics for Actris.
We display proofs and proof states taken directly from the Coq
mechanisation, which differ in notation from the paper as shown in
Figure

.

10.1.
Mechanisation effort

The mechanisation of Actris is built on top of the mechanisation of Iris

KJB+ 17
,
JKBD 16
,
JKJ+ 18
.
To carry out proofs in separation logic, we use the MoSeL Proof Mode
(formerly Iris Proof Mode)
KTB 17
,
KJJ+ 18
, which
provides an embedded proof assistant for separation logic in Coq.
Building Actris on top of the Iris and MoSeL framework in Coq has a number of tangible
advantages:

•

By defining channels on top of HeapLang,
we do not have to define a full programming language
semantics, and can reuse all of the program libraries and Coq machinery,
including the tactics for symbolic execution of non message-passing programs.

•

Since Actris is mechanised as an Iris library
we get all of the features of Iris for free,
such as the ghost state mechanisms for reasoning about concurrency.

•

When proving the Actris proof rules, we can make use of the MoSeL Proof
Mode to carry out proofs directly using separation logic, thus reasoning at
a high level of abstraction.

•

We can make use of the extendable nature of the MoSeL Proof Mode to
define custom tactics for symbolic execution of message-passing programs.

These advantages made it possible to mechanise Actris, along with the examples of
the paper, with a small Coq development of a
total size of about 5000 lines of code (comments and whitespace included).
The line count of the different components are shown in
Figure

22
.

Sections

\sim

–

)

10.2.
Tactic support for session type-based reasoning

To carry out interactive Actris proofs using symbolic execution, we follow the
methodology described in the original Iris Proof Mode paper
KTB 17
.
In particular, this means that the logic in Coq is presented in weakest precondition
style rather than using Hoare triples.
For handling
send
or
recv
we define the following tactics:

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
wp_send

(
t1

..

tn
)

with

"[
H1

..

Hn
]"
and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
wp_recv

(
y1

..

yn
)

as

"
H
"
.

These tactics roughly perform the following actions:

•

Find a
send
or
recv
in evaluation position of the program
under consideration.

•

Find a corresponding
c\rightarrowtail\mathit{prot}
hypothesis in the separation
logic context.

•

Normalise the protocol
\mathit{prot}
using the rules for duals, composition,
recursion, and swapping so it has a

\textnormal{{!}}\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
or

\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}\,\langle v\rangle{\left\{P\right\}}.\,\mathit{prot}
construct in its head position.

•

In case of
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
wp_send
, instantiate the variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
using
the terms
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
(
t1

..

tn
)
, and create a goal for the proposition

P
with the hypotheses
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
[
H1

..

Hn
]
.
Hypotheses prefixed with
\mathtt{\$}
will automatically be consumed to resolve a
subgoal of
P
if possible.
In case the terms
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
(
t1

..

tn
)
are omitted, an attempt is made to
determine these using unification.

•

In case of
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
wp_recv
, introduce the variables
\vec{x}\!:\!\vec{\tau}
into the
context by naming them
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
(
y1

..

yn
)
, and create a hypothesis

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
H
for
P
.

The implementation of these tactics follows the approach by Krebbers
et al.

KTB 17
.
The protocol normalisation is implemented via logic programming with type
classes.

As an example we will go through a proof of the following program:

\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{\ } l @{}}\mathtt{prog\_ref\_swap\_loop}:={}\lambda\_.&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{c}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{start}\ {\thinspace}(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} rec}\thinspace{\mathit{go}}\ {c^{\prime}}\mathrel{:=}\begin{array}[t]{@{} l @{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace l\mathrel{:=}{\texttt{recv}\ c^{\prime}}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
l\leftarrow\mathop{!}l+2;\\
\texttt{send}\ c^{\prime}\ ();\ \mathit{go}\ c^{\prime})\ \texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
\end{array}\\
&\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{l_{1}}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{1}8\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} let}\thinspace{l_{2}}\mathrel{:=}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} ref}\;{2}0\thinspace\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1} in}\\
&\texttt{send}\ c\ l_{1};\ \texttt{send}\ c\ l_{2};\\
&\texttt{recv}\ c;\ \texttt{recv}\ c;\\
&\mathop{!}l_{1}+\mathop{!}l_{2}\end{array}

Here, the forked-off thread acts as a service that recursively receives locations,
adds 2 to their stored number, and then sends back a flag indicating that the
location has been updated.
The main thread, acting like a client, first allocates two new references,
to
18
and
20
, respectively, which are both sent to the service
after which the update flags are received.
It finally dereferences the updated locations, and adds their values together,
thus returning
42
.
To verify this program, we use the following recursive protocol:

\mathtt{prot\_ref\_loop}\triangleq\mu(\mathit{rec}:\mathsf{iProto}).\thinspace\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(x:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\mapsto x\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\mapsto x+2\right\}}.\,\mathit{rec}

The (forked-off) service follows the (dual of) the protocol exactly,
while the main thread follows a weakened version.
The recursion is unfolded
twice, after which the second send has been swapped ahead of the first receive,
allowing it to first send both values before receiving:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{\ } l}\mathtt{prot\_ref\_loop}\sqsubseteq&\begin{array}[t]{@{} l}\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{1}:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(x_{1}:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle\ell_{1}\rangle{\left\{\ell_{1}\mapsto x_{1}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell_{2}:\kern-0.50003pt\mathsf{Loc})(x_{2}:\mathbb{Z})\,\langle\ell_{2}\rangle{\left\{\ell_{2}\mapsto x_{2}\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell_{1}\mapsto(x_{1}+2)\right\}}.\\
\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell_{2}\mapsto(x_{2}+2)\right\}}.\,\mathtt{prot\_ref\_loop}\end{array}\end{array}

The full Coq proof of the program is shown in
Section

10.2
.

10.3.
Tactic support for subprotocols

While the Actris tactics automatically apply the subprotocol rules during
symbolic execution, as shown in
Section

10.2
,
we sometimes want to prove subprotocol relations as explicit lemmas.
We have tactic support for such proofs as well.
We extend the existing MoSeL tactics

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iIntros
,
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iExists
,
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iFrame
,
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iModIntro
,
and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iSplitL
/
iSplitR
to automatically use the subprotocol rules to
turn the goal into an equivalent goal where the regular Iris tactics apply.

•

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iIntros

(
x1

..

xn
)

"
H1

..

Hm
"

transforms the subprotocol goal to
begin with
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
n
universal quantification and
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
m
implications, using the rules

11
and
11
,
and then introduces the quantifiers (naming them
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
x1

..

xn
) into the
Coq context, and the hypotheses (naming them
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
H1

..

Hm
) into the
separation logic context.

•

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iExists

(
t1

..

tn
)
transforms the subprotocol goal to start with

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
n
existential quantifiers, using the

11
,
11
and
11
rules,
and then instantiates these quantifiers with the terms
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
t1

..

tn
specified by the pattern.

•

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iFrame

"
H
"
transforms the subprotocol goal into a separating
conjunction between the payload predicates of the head symbols of either
protocol, using the rules

11
and
11
,
and then tries to solve the payload predicate subgoal using
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
"
H
"
.

•

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iModIntro
transforms the subprotocol goal into a
goal starting with a later modality (
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
), using the rules

11
and
11
,
and then introduces that later by stripping off a later from any hypothesis
in the separation logic context.

•

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iSplitL
/
iSplitR

"
H1

..

Hn
"
transforms the subprotocol goal into a
separating conjunction between the payload predicates of the head symbols of either
protocol, using the

11
,
11
and
11
rules,
and then creates two subgoals.
For
\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iSplitL
the left subgoal is given the hypotheses

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
H1

..

Hn
from the separation logic context,
while the right subgoal is given any remaining hypotheses, and
vice versa
for

\lst
@ifdisplaystyle
iSplitR
.

The extensions of these tactics are implemented by defining custom type class
instances that hook into the existing MoSeL tactics as described by Krebbers
et al.

KTB 17
.

⬇
@ifdisplaystyle

1

Figure 24

Proof of subprotocol relation

To demonstrate these tactics, we will go through a proof of the subprotocol relation
for the list reversing service presented in

6.3
:

\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{} l @{}}&\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.42502pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{v}:\mathsf{List}\ \mathsf{Val})\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{v}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\\
\sqsubseteq&\textnormal{{!}}\,(\ell:\kern-0.42502pt\mathsf{Loc})(\vec{x}:\mathsf{List}\ T)\,\langle\ell\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\right\}}.\,\textnormal{{?}}\!\,\langle()\rangle{\left\{\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\mathsf{reverse}\ \vec{x}\right\}}.\,\mathbf{end}\end{array}

Recall that the following conversion between
the list representation predicate with payload

\texttt{Hlr}\hskip 8.50012pt:\hskip 8.50012pt\textstyle\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}_{I_{T}}\vec{x}\ \mathrel{\ast\!\!{-}\!\!\ast}\ (\exists\vec{v}.\hskip 1.7pt\ell\overset{\scalebox{.35}{list}}{\mapsto}\vec{v}\ast\Sep_{(x,v)\in(\vec{x},\vec{v})}.I_{T}\ x\ v)
